===== 0002601_19060728_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
ES THE McDOUGALL'S Pategt SELF-RAISING FLOUR BINTASTRY. ' The Examiner, Saturday, July 28, 1906 ,1 WOMAN'S REALM. L(Gopriyht• )

were no longer mosuy ot an alien mne was no proud and separate creature, set apart by. destiny. Fate meant her for a like burden witla her fellows ; wifehood and the mystery of mother- hood might soon be hers. And yet how unlike she was to these, in that she had been chosen for a mate by one as far above the common man as a pine above the spare and struggling grass. This was her own especial glory. • For this dear grace of God's she owed deathless thanks. An unhappier lot might have made her think she loved some other man when she knew not what love really, was. She saw how possible it was to take brass for gold in ignorance, and was ignorantly sure that Richard was fine gold indeed. Now that she had put her arms about his neck and felt his lips on hers she hardly feared for hint in this wild adventure of the hateful desert. Her mother had desired to part them, and that very desire had brought them • closer together. Her defeat was so overwhelming that Madge pitied and iorgave her. She appeared inconsiderable, futile, and now too insignificant in the heavenly order of things to be taken quite seriously. Her work in the world was done ; her child's task but begiriaing That the mother desired an absurd and ridiculous marriage, now that such a marriage became utterly impossible, was a sign of actual senility. For perhaps the first time in her fighting life Madge said "poor mother," and was conscious, of warm virtue in her own gentleness. The ' mother was now a portion of the past; for the time being it was easy to forget her more bitter faults. All the love which by the divinest right should have been the mother's turned in one stream to the father. How greatly he delighted in his child Madge knew by instant and inevitable intuition; she saw it written in mystic characters Which she now could read. She knew others could love, and what her father's life had been she guessed sorrow- fully. And how sweet and good he was to her, who did not deserve it! With him she was conscious of her failings ; she feared his censure when she was ready to provoke her mother's. For one impulsive moment she was inclined to tell him all she had done for Richard. That she did not, made her even more affectionate.

"Oh, what shall I do without you, Madge l" "You will never be without me, dad." But he shook his head. For him India was like the ,Soudan. - It would swallow up all he really loved,

The-Mother that Madge had forgiven during that drive home shewed she was not to be forgotten during the next few days, and she soon ran up a new score with her visionary child. For Madge lived in a dream and stared at people about her with inward eyes. She became silent and self- absorbed, and started on being spoken to. Very often Mrs. Gretton ,had to speak twice; and ever on the second time her voice ran up the scale, and was acute and piercing. And that Madge treated her with marked consideration, never retorting, annoyed her very much. It was a thing she had not been accustomed to; she missed an occupation. If Madge stayed in her place, she lacked the joy of putting her there. She felt like the Punjab Frontier Force when peace has lasted a miserable long year. And on the third day peace was broken. The cause was Mr. Robert Maitland.

Up to this time Madge had treated him with good-humoured contempt, softened by the feeling no girl can fail to have for the man who appears to love, and is obviously ready to marry her. But now she began to be sorry for him, and could no longer be cruel. Her loyalty to Richard, moreover, was almost fanatical; she had become sacred; it behoved her now to act with infinite circumspection; she was ready to go into a conventual retreat until her true lover came to call her. And her mother's clumsy references to Bobby Outraged her soul. It was time that something was done. (She proceeded to do it, hot, as of old, with fear and trembling, and audacity beautifully mingled, but with a -dignity which she *as well aware became her and annoyed her mother past endurance. "I think, mother," she began, "that you had better not ask Mr. Maitland for Christmas.

Mrs. Gretton lifted her nose and her glasses at the smile time, and surveyed her daughter for a long minute. "You think—that I had better not ask—Mr. Maitland for Christmas, do you ? " "Yes, mother," replied Madge, with outward coldness.

"And why, may I ask?" "Because it's no good, that's why," said Madge, almost sulkily, relapsing into her more ancient manner. "And may I be permitted to inquire. what, in your opinion, is no good ? " asked Mrs. Gretton, still with her glasses over her sharp and shining nose.

_ "You know what I mean," cried Madge.• "Would to Heaven that I did!" retorted her mother. "Would to Heaven that I did! But when I see you acting thus to a most estimable young man, to the most estimable young man I know, I cannot declare with any regard for truth that I understand in the least what you mean." Madge stared at her. "What do you mean by saying 'acting thus,' mother?"

Mrs. Gretton lowered her glasses and folded her hands.

"lam not to be interrogated as to what I mean. When I said acting thus,' I meant what I said, as 1 am accustomed to do."

Madge got up. i "You don't mean to imply— "How dare you!" said Mrs. Gretton. "How dare you suggest I mean to imply anything? And you have not yet explained why you venture tc dictate to me whom I shall ask to the house at Christmas."

Madge shook her head impatiently. "I didn't dictate, and I don't want to, mother. But I want you to know that I do not want Mr. Maitland here. And it's no good." Her mother bit her lips. "Then, if lam forced to guess what you mean by mere slang, I opine you have changed your mini, Margaret. And may I ask what has induced you to do so ?"

--"---I haven't changed my mind," said Madge, indignantly, "and—. "Then if you have not changed it, why niake this scene?" asked her mother, with a fixed and glassy smile, which reduced Madge to utter desperation. "Well, then," she cried, "if you make me say it, I mean I won't marry Mr. Robert Maitland, and I wouldn't if he was the last man in the world." Madge's dignity had covered her like a cloak for exactly thirty seconds. Her mother felt more equal to the combat, and rose to her feet. But Madge did not quail, for Richard was always behind her now, and Duke-street, St James's, was within easy reach. "May I venture to inquire if Mr. Robert Mait- land has asked you to nim:ry him ? " "You know he hasn't," cried Madge, angrily, "but he always looks as if he would, and I won't. So there!" _ _

. " She says so there 'to her mother—to me I" said Mrs. Gretton, looking at the unanswering ceiling. "Well, then, you shouldn't encourage him to worry me so. For I won't marry him. And it's not kind of you to let him think I will."

Mrs. Gretlon sat down again and groaned.

"Good Heavens! To think I live a daughter who defies me, flouts me, insults me, when I wish for nothing, think of nothing, work for nothing but her good!" Madge sniffed. "I'm all right as I am, mother, and I don't want anyone to work for my good; if it's to marry Bobby Maitland, at lest." "Who has asked you to marry him, you imper- tinent chid? " asked her mother, now in an obvious rage, for her hands trembled and her face was flushed in patches. "Who has asked you to marry him? And I have been so good to you. I have not told your father about your scandalous and disgraceful conduct. And this is my reward! Oh, If I believed—"

"What ? " cried Madge, anogrrer imother came to

her. "Are you thinking about that? ' Oh, no, it cannot be, it cannot be!" But Madge knew her mother was referring to Richard, and she flushed scarlet and then turned pal "I'll think about anyone I like," she cried. panting, "but not about Bobby Mai hand. And you can tell father just what you want." ' "Your father would die of shame ! Ile would die of shame!" But Madge laughed. , "Oh, no, he wouldn't. I'll tell him myself. He's always kind to me." "What ?" And Madge sat down. "Why can't you leave me 'alone ? " site cried, forgetting that she had herself provoked the scene. " You're always bullying me, and I won't be bullied. Ind life at home is miserable. If it wasn't for father, I'd run away and never, never come back again." She burst into tears, and sobbed till the sofa .d.nrar

"I'M bullying her! .aullying! And her home is miserable ! And she•il run away!" Mrs. Gretton moaned, and, retreating to an ottoman, fell into its cushions. Presently her groans were so heartre ,ding that Madge looked up. " Qh. don't, 'mother !" she sQiueaked.__ __ •

• • •OI—I will !".said Mrs. Gretton, sobbing. .If I desire to weep, who shall stay me?" But as her tears had no very obvious effect on Madge, she presently dried them delicately, and looked in much better social condition ,than her daughter, who had really been crying. "Do you desire that I should have the shame of telling your father about your conduct concerning that foolish, that lamentable affair, which doubt- less you must now regret ? " " N—no, " said Madge; "but I—l. don' t regret it." "Oh, you are shameless!" cried her mother. "But whether you regret it or not, I regret it \for you. It has caused me many sleepless hours, it has made me ill; it has made me weep through the watches of the long, long nights. And do not imagine though I say nothing, that lam unmindful of your welfare. My eye is always upon you ;my protecting care, so far as my health allows is always :bout you. Ungrateful as you are for a mother's unselfish love, you do not know all I have done, and am prepared to do, to save you from yourself."

But this was more than Madge could stand. She jumped up with blazing eyes. , "I do know, I do know what you have done. And it won't be any good." Mrs. Gretton started, but, controlling herself, raised her glasses. . _ .

"Oh, it ;on% be any good, Margaret? Please explain NA hat will not be any good!" Madge choked, and desired to scream. But tears came to her relief.

"Oh, you've sent him away!" she cried. "You have sent him away You and that horrible Nr Dan have sent him away!" "Good Heavens! And how does she know that'?" thought Mrs. Gretton. "To whom are you referring, Margaret?" she asked, in her most awful tones.

"You know very well," sobbed Madge. "But he'll come back again; I know he'll come back!" "Good Heavens 1" cried Mrs. Gretton., "Do you

mean—"

"Yes, I mean Captain Blundell— I do mean him. And you know I mean him !" screamed Madge. "And you've sent him into the Soudan, and if he dies I'll never speak to you aga4 Oh, never, never!"

She fell on the sofa and sobbed dreadfully, while her mother stared at her, and wondered how it was she had guessed the truth. But that she had dared to mention his name was not to be borne./ "You wicked girl=" "I'm not wicked," cried Madge i "it's you that are wicked and cruel. Oh, I'll not speak to you I" "You shall speak to me," said her mother, furiously. "What do you know about this man? Tell me I "

But in Madge's mind were awful pictures of the desert; she saw the red simoon and beheld vultures wheeling in a pitiless sky, under which toiled her exiled and faithful lover.

"For I love him," she 'said to herself. Richard V

Her mother was an alien, an enemy, a hostile influence dividing loving hearts. Her voice was harsh and hateful, her touch repulsive. She felt it on her shoulder now, and shook herself free. Then suddenly some dignity returned to her in her despair. "Mother, leave me alone," she cried; "you can say nothing that is any good to me or you. For you hive sent him away. And I'll never, never marry any other man in the world." Then suddenly the mother knew that her child was become a woman; she was no longer to be led or driven; her future lay in the lap of the gods. And for that knoment at least the elder woman hated her daughter bitterly.

If Ifedge in the first flush of delight at her lover's vivid passion had half forgiven her mother for what she had done, being assured it would in the end enhance Richard in the eyes of the world, now, after this conflict, she withdrew her pardon. Who but an enemy could have used such words or have spoken in a voice the very tone of which was an insult ? And if, for some incredible reason, such as desire of a marriage advantageous on worldly grounds, she was ready to sacrifice her child's happiness, it was her own wishes, not her daughter's, that she thought of. Therefore, she was selfish, and not only selfish, but unscrupulous. Madge saw dimly that the sense of defeat spurred her mothe; to renewed combat, and, knowing how she would take defeat herself, she viewed her fox ever with increasing suspicion. But if the immediate future lay with Fate, s decision final for years might in the end rest with her father. Madge knew that there was a limit of endurance in him. ant limit had never been reached by her mother, but his daughter felt that any interference with her happiness might find him an unsuspected reef in a smiling sea. And now he was her secret ally. What could detach him from her even for a time?

She answered that question when she went into his smoking-room that evening, and broke her mother's chief weapon in pieces. She sat down and told him all that she had done for 'Richard; she omitted nothing. "Why did you not tell me this before ? " he asked, sternly. "I was afraid. No, I was not afraid, But what good was it? " "And why do you tell me now ? " No subtlety or evasion could serve her like the truth, and that her instinct taught her. But hex air was half-innocent.

, "To prevent mother doing it." He did not speak for a minute. And the reason he did not was simple; he found he had nothing to say. Astonishment took away his breatil ;he was incredulous, And yet desired to laugh. And that Lord Graham had laughed was a great relief to him, as Madge knew it would be 'when- she insisted on it ;n her hurried story. It gave a serious matter the aspect of a huge joke ; it left him almost help- less. But even more than that it was his wonder at the child of yesterday being the woman of to-day. "Good Lord " he said at last. "Well—l'm

He stared at her curiously, and threw a cigar, which he had allowed to go out, into the grate. She kept her eyes upon the fire's red heart. "And Fielding?"

"He was—furious. And he's never spoken, to me since."

--She looked meek and sorrowful, and spoke as if she regretted it.

It Oh, dad, are you very angry?" • "It's not the word," he murmuvd, as he eyed her forlornly. Would a man be angry at an earthquake? Or M anything which changed the face of nature? "What's the good of being angry ? " he asked himself. "0 Madge!" She slipped down upon the hearthrug, and leant against his knee. I did try everything else, dad. And poor Richard was so unhappy at not having anything to do which was worth doing." "Then you are not sorry, Madge ?" IShe looked up at hini, and then looked down. "Not if you're not angry." i "But if lam F"

"I—can't be," whispered Madge, "because—" And the father sighed. But he let his hand rest

-aI to be very angry. Tror it was very wrong of you, Madge. And did Graham laugh. really!" „

Midge nodded. "He just shouted." "And you ? " "I cried. And I asked him to be sure and give Richard the D. 5.0., and he said if he deserved it he would have it. So it's all right." . . .

"I'm not at all surprised lie laughed," cried Gretton, a little drily. I don't imagine he often plays a part in such a comedy in Pall Mall. "It was a comedy then, dad?" He admitted by silence that so far it had turned out one.

"And you won't tell mother I told you?" "Why not? "Because—" said Madge, hesitating. "Why?" Madge blinked. ' "Well, because if you don't, she'll think she can get you on her side at any time. And if you do, she'll be trying all she can to make you take her part. And it will be very, very uncomfortable for you." "And—" said Gretton, with knitted brows. ' "And for me. Because she might do it. No,l say she couldn't." "I'm afraid I've gone a little too far," ka answered, doubtfully. (To be continued.)

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Royalty at Goodwood and Cowes,—The Duke of Richmond will be assisted by his young unmarried daughter, Lady Helen Gordon-Lennox, and by his sister Lady Caroline Gordon-Lennox, in dispensing the hospitalities of Goodwood House, where the King will be a guest for the race week. The Queen will not go to Goodwood but will join the King on Board the Royal yacht Victoria and Albert, for the Cowes regetta week in the Solent. The Majesties will live on board but the King will spend 3 good deal of the time on his racing cutter" Britannia." His Majesty has asked for plans for anew racing yacht, which be is contemplating having built and fitted out for next season. Mr. Chamberlain's Wedding.—Mr. Austen Chamber- lain's marriage to Miss ivy Dundas drew an immense attendance to St. Margaret's, Westminster. The throng of people and the many beautiful and costly presents received by thebride andbriclegroore,constitutedrilmost a record in the history of Parliamentary weddings, and there was a remarkable gathering of prominent politicans of all parties. Unhappily Mrloseph Chamber- lain was prevented by a severe attack of gout from being present, which was a great disappointment to himself and family, and the many hundreds of guests who attended the reception. Mr. ard Mrs. Austen Chamberlain are spending the first part of the honey- moon at " Camilla Lacy "—Dorking. The place DOW belongs to M. Leverton Harris, but it is famous as the house which the authoress Fanny Burney built, with the proceeds of her much criticised novel " She had sprung into fame with her first book "Evelina" which appeared in 1778 but her genius was short lived, and although she received a larger sum for "Camilla " than had ever been paid for a novel, its literary demerits ruined her reputation as a writer. The late Lady Curzon.—The American coloey in London was largely represented at the memorial service of Lady Curzon, who was one of the most popular of the group`of American women who have settled in Europe. She had never been really well since her third daughter was born in March 1904, and her family did not approve of her return to India after her all but fatal illness at Weimer Castle. The climate tried her, but, although her health had long given cause for anxiety, her death came as a great shock to a wide circle cl friends. Lady Curzon was born in 1803, and at that time her father, the late Mr. Levi Leiter was in the drapery business, in Chicago. He however retired early in life, one of the richest men in America—and moved to Washington where the 1 eiters became one of the HOT-WEATHER WOES leading families in Society. Lady Curzon was married in 1895,and leaves three little daughters,the eldest being, Weak & Languid Every Summer; ten years old. DRESS-FASHION-HOME

CHILDREN AT GARDEN PARTIES.

Few garden parties appear to be considered complete nowadays without a fair contingent of children of all ages; in fact, the young invites nowadays look upon this form of entertainment as almost their own prerogative. As a conse- quence their toilets are a matter of serious con- sideration to the nursery authorities, for, al- though over-dressing in the matter of juvenil& attire can hardly be looked upon as one of the modistic sins of the present season, a certain amount of elaboration is tecessary. Some dainty little frocks of white mercerised lawn have been made of late, the skirts of which are trimmed with bands of hand-painted muslin, many women whose talents- lie in this direction designing charming schemes of wreaths of roses, forget-me- nots, and daisies. White muslin is also frequently adorned with frills edged with fine Malines lace, embroidered in washing. silks, and run through with bibe ribbon.

A CHARMING DRESS.

From tusgoro silk or Shantung may be evolved the most charming dresses. One depicted in this column is relieved with touches of some bright contrasting colmir such as pale blue or cerise velvet or satin ribbon, or whatever colour is most becoming to the Wearer. The full bodice

opens in a small V-shape at the peek, tbe open- ing being bordered with the coloured velvet or ribbon. The material on the shoulders is ar- ranged in small neat tucks, and there are bretelles of coarse lace insertion, which are cen- tinued down beneath the belt as far as the tucks that trim the hem of the skirt. At the waist the fulness of the bodice is .drawn in with a pretty belt of the ribbon, each row finishing in a small bow. The sleeves are trimmed with the lace insertion, running from the shoulder to the cuff at the elbow, the cuff itself being of the inser- tion, and finished with a narrow turn-back cuff cal velvet or satin to match the ribbon.

DiazrrHr Mtreux BLouss.

Depicted in our illustration we have a dainty little blouse in book muslin, glorified by many and various tucks. The pointed yoke is com- posed of minute tuckings 'laid vertically, and is edged with bebe Valenciennes lace. The lower or "skirt" part of the blouse is,tucked horizon- tally, the tucks being distinctly wider here than in the yoke. They follow the pointed line of the yoke outline, and where they meet in the centre are placed medallion insets of Valenciennes lace edged with frilling,s. The sleeves are composed of two puffs, separated by a band of tucks, and finished at the c".lyrs, with lace frills. The day of endless transparencies is at an end, and now slips of some description are usually worn under thin blouses. A good silk slip or a creamy satin

one is a valuable possession, for it doubles the value of any blouse under which it is worn; and in these latter days, when most summer frock.a are mounted over white, one slip will serve for r.ll blouses, and perhaps a dress or two as welL

A WELL-KNOWN SCOTTISH WRITER.

Readers of the Woman at Home, the People's Friend, and many other magazines and journals will learn with regret of the early death of that gifted writer, •" Hannah Drown Mackenzie." Mrs. Welsh, who won her literary reputation under her maiden name, was the daughter of John Mackenzie, of Dochcarron in Ross-shire, and of Georgina Brown. Through her mother she was directly • descended from the author of " Brown's Bible," and nearly related to Dr. John Brown, who wrote "Rab and his Friends." At, the aze of fourteen she was publishing verses in the Poet's Corner of the Glasgow Weekly Herald. At sixteen she did work for the review. ing columns of the Glasgow Herald. Like many other Scottish writers, her first serious attempts in fiction were published in the People's Friend, to which she contributed aerial stories. Her best known books are "After Touch of Wedded Hearts," "Crowned Victor," and "Hector Macrae."

By MORLEY ROBERTS,

',Author of "The Westena Avernus," " Strong ' Men and True," "The Ea:rth Mothcr, " "Red Earth," "The Great Jester," &o,

CHAPTER XXII.

, She fell upon her knees by her bed and prayed I to Heaven and to Him, and when she rose she was quiet, and, as it seemed, very, very old. Ifer mother; her very father' were of a strange world. She had left them, and belonged body and soul and spirit to this, her departing lover. What there was to bear she could endure, but let him lift his little finger, and not all the chains in the family could hold her. What was anything weighed taminst this strong man's love? She, recognised far more easily than a girl with- out insight into soldiering could have done how difficult it would be for Richard to refuse the position now offered him. That it must ,be dangerous she knew, in spite of the way her lover : softened down its hazards, and that was the very reason he must accept it. But how came it tO be offered him at all? How was it that Major-General Turner knew he was in England? A poor captain comes home with no public F houting, and 'nothing had been said of his arrival in any paper. When Madre had got so far she found it easy to go further. She and Richard owed this to Sir Dan and her mother who had conspired together. That explained Mrs. Gretton's malicious smile as nothing else could do; it explained the reason of their not now going back to Gretton Park. How cruel end cowardly it was of them. They had plotted .her lover'edeath ; they were no better than murdi revs. How triumphant her mother would he! Knowing nothing of their haviug met, she would believe they could not meet now. • "Does she think I am a child still? asked Madge, bitterly, as she passed her mother's door. "I will shew her that I am a woman." She met her father in the hall and went up to him straight. He was looking very serious. "Has Richard written to you, too?" she asked, staring him in the face. "Yes,' Madge.",_ "Come into your room, I nant to speak to you," she said, in a low strained voice. She was very white and her eyes wen) heavy- lidded. _

"Oh, my Oear!" said her father, taking her in his arms. She kissed him and freed herself. "Mother has done this," she cried. "Mother and Sir Dan; I know it." • "How ?," asked Grotto-rt. "I know it. She has tried to kill him. Perhaps he will be killed. I will never forgive her." She looked wild and spoke wildly. "Hush!" said her father. "I will not hush," cried Madge. "Father, if this is true I will never speak to her again till he comes back safe or I know he is deal" "You don't mean it, Madge ? " "Oh, you don't know me either," said Madge, bitterly. "Can't you see I'm not a child any more?"

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Rewsey-road Wesleyan Church. The services at this church on Sunday wore well- attended. In the morning the preacher was the Rev, J. Manning who based a powerful discourse on the words: —" I went by the field of the slothful,and by the vine- yard of the man void of understanding ; and, 10, it was all grown over with thorns, and nettles had covered the face thereof, and the stone wall thereof was broken down. Then I saw, and considered it well ; I looked upon it and received instruction. Yet a little sleep, a little slumber, a little folding of the hands to sleep; so shall thy poverty come as one that travelleth, and thy want as an armed man" (Proverbs xxiv., 30 to 34). At the evening service Mr. Peake, a young local preacher, gave an excellent sermon on the text: " Then Agrippa said unto Paul, Almost thou persuadest me to be a Christian ' " (Acts xxvi., 28.) The choir effectively rendered the anthem " Lead kindly light " Mr. W. Pearoe presiding at the organ. Christ Church, Padgate. The annual services on behalf of the day and Sunday schools were held MI Christ Church, Padgate, on Sunday. Tne morning preacher was the Rev. B. Percival, vicar of St. Peter's, Warrington, who made a very strong appeal for definite religious teaching in both classes of school. Re pointed out the great sacri- fices that churchpeople had made on behalf of their schools, mentioning the fact that since 1870 they had raised over thirty millions sterling for school building, - whereas they had the option of having Board Schools built by the rates, had they been satisfied with the religious teaching which would have been given within their walls. He deplored the fact -that the teaching in the Wairington Secondary School was of a purely secular character and appealed for the unity of the various denominations in order that a scheme of religious %relit might be drawn up and taught to the pupils of this institution. Ihe preacher in the evening was the Rev. S. E. Dymott, vicar of St. ". Bridget's, Wavertree, eho also pointed out the unsatis- factory condition of the present Education Bill from a Churchman's point of view. In the morning Simper's anthem," 0 how amiable " was rendered by the choir, while in the evening the anthem rendered was " Let Clod arise "by the same composer. Mr. F. Blackwell presided at the organ.

Rev. W. Bracecamp Declines Important Living. The Rev. W. Bracecamp bad been offered and has declined the living of Christ Church, Attercliffe, Sheffield, rendered vacant by the resignation of the Rev. Geo. Depledge, who has held it for 35 years. The net value of the living is £6OO. Preaching in St. Paul's Chnreh, on Sunday evening from the words :—" Trust in the Lord with all thino heart, and lean not unto thine own understanding. In all thy ways acknowledge Him, and He shall direct thy' , path,'; the Rev. W. Bracecamp said :—No doubt some of • you have seen from the local newspapers that a pressing ( invitation has been given me to accept an importarl position in Sheffield—a position I never sought, in fact I did not know that the living was vacant until Tuesday morning, when, among my other letters came 0110 from the patron of the living asking me to carry on God's work in a very large centre of industry with a population of from 20,000 to 22,000 with a very large church—and altogether lin important sphere of work. My decision has alread,v been given, as on my way to church this evening '- posted a letter to the patron of the living telling ; 40. that I could not see my way to accept the offer gave him my reasons. I quite remembered ' tha „ four years ago you were good enough to invite me '" become the Vicar of this parish. I was unknown to the majority of you, and there was practically a unanimous vote that I should come amongst you, and be your Vicar. You placed confidence in me,and, therefore,l con- sider that four years is too short a time in which to give you a return for the confidence you then placed in me. Perhaps some of you may think I am making a mistake, but I did not consider the extra money and position. I tried to leave money in the background, and to look at the matter in the light that half a loaf in the place where one is happy and surrounded by a congregation that will support one, as this congregation supports me. is worth a whole loaf where there is no happiness, aud where there may not be the support I am receiving here. I ask for your confidence in the future as I have bad it in the past, and I ask you to help me all you possibly can, and give me credit for trying to do my duty to you. The announcement of the refusal of the living gave the congregation great pleasure, and both Mr. and Mrs. Bracecamp were heartily thanked at the conclusion of the service for the decision they had arrived at.

A PAMPERED POODLE.

Some of the items in an account which a high.class- West End veterinary surgeon sought to recover at the Marylebone County Court an Tuesday for attendance on a Nevern-square gentleman's pet fox terrier included whiskey, beef tea, and eggs. The dog was taken into plaintiff's infirmary with a broken thigh, another veterinary surgeon was called in for consultation, and an operation baying been found necessary, the pampered creature was given chloroform. The claim in all was .Cl 7 odd, and Judge Selfe allowed plaintiff £ll 17s. 6d.

The origin of the custom of firing a volley over a Roidier's grave is connected with the ancient belief that evil spirits were kept away from a body by making a great noise. Of this we have an example in the "passing hell." After the invention of firerarms, the. latilp &Wilke became emblednaiical of the Wo nrofession—of his baying fallen amidst the sounds of battle.

A ra—ier curious history is attached to the march-past of the Ist Dragoon Guards. The quick-step which is known as "Raditzky" is really the marth-past of the Raditzky Hussars, an Austrian cavalry coupe. It was adopted by the let Dragoon Guards at the instigation of their•foreign bandmaster, Who claimed to be ite composer, but it was really written by the cele- brated Johann Strauss.

Mr. Fletcher Moulton, K.C., M.P., is one of the -meat- remarkable men at. the Bar. Born in 1844, the son of a Wesleyan minister, he waa Senior Wrangler and Smith's Prizetnan at Cam- bridge in 1868, was President of the Union, and, not content with these honours, he became a gold medallfst of London University. He had not been long at the Bar when he became a leading authority on patent law, in which his rare mathematical and scientific ability gave him pre- eminence. •

Frederick William I. of Prussia used to spend many hilarious hours it the Tobacco Club, where his chief delight was to make his guests the butt of his practical jokes. A favourite victim was Jacob von Gundling, •an eminent scientist, whom on one occasion his majesty ordered to read aloud certain abusive articles about himself which the Bing had sent to the daily papers; while by his side was placed a monkey dressed in exact imitation of the pro- fessor, which he was compelled to enibrace as his brother.

The story of how Sir George Kekewich left Oxford is amusing. He was at Bailiol and rose there to the highest eminence as an oarsman and a racket-player. But he had no taste for an appearancepa:tbytnfw dinoa,aye ,ifDhHio chatiel, and during his last term never once put in an attendance. The result was that the Dons sent him down for three days. This annoyed him so much that he shook the dust of Oxford for ever off his feet, withdrew his "caution" money, which amounted to £2ooff the college books, and went to spend it in feasting in London. Thus he returned to his parents with- out having taken a degree.

The Victoria Cross was first issued by a Royal Warrant dated January 29, 1856, for the pur- pose of rewarding individual acts of bravery. Any additional act of bravery is recorded by a oar attached to the ribbon by which the cross is suspended. A pension of £lO a year is oestowed upon non-commissioned officers end men only who receive the cross, and a furthis pension of £5 is given with each extra bar.

ST. ELPHIN'S CLERGY SCHOOLS.

The Prize Distribution.

On Tuesday at St. Flphin's School, Darley Dale, the Bishop of Liverpool distributed 200 prizes and certifi- cates won by the daughters of the clergy in the dioceses of Liverpool, Chester, and Manchester. There was a large attendance. The Bishop in his address, stated that the school was now under far better conditions than when at Warrington. There had only been one case of infectious disease at the school during the year and that was so quickly isolated that it did not spread. Through the kindliness of Canon Willis a chapel had been added to the premises during the year. A new organ would shortly be introduced into the school, and towards that something like £4O more was required to cl.ar off the cost A new sanatorium was greatly needed, and for this to be up-to-date £l,OOO would be required, and be hoped that sum would be raised without encroaching on their present flnancies. He congratulated Miss Kennedy and her staff on the success the school had attained, and he was pleased to state that it was uow under the Board of Education as a training school for elementary school mistresses. The school bad attained its highest position in numbers during the past year. He urged the students to have for their ideal the sundial, and for their motto "Ad Lucern (applause.)—lt should be stated that grants amounting to £2,065 were made to the Widows and Orphans' Fund. The income arising from investments was £833, collections £336, subscrip- tions £692, donations £l5O. During the past year Dorothy Temperley had gained the Goldsmiths' Com- pany Scholarship of £6O a year to Girton. Miss Flood, one of the teachers, bad gained her M.A., degree at the Dublin University. After tea the students gave Roeckel's cantata" The Hours." in a very praiseworthy manner

The Examiner, Saturday, July 28, 1906.

PICTURESQUE PAGEANT. THE FANCY DRESS PARADE AND

CARNIVAL AT WARRINGTON.

Splendid Effort for the Infirmary.

Satui•day saw the happy consummation of many week's untiring exertion on the part of those who had promoted the fancy dress parade and carnival on behalf of that great and deserving charity—the Infirmary. A splendid display! was the verdict of the thousands of delighted spectators who thronged the'reute. And, withoya doubt, the pro- cession was one that would have done ciedit to any town. It came as a striking and pleasant contrast to the usual prosaicness of town life and filled the streets with colour and gaiety. Had there been no other object but to please and amuse the townspeople the spectacle would have been welcomed. But how much more gratifying it was to know that this great array of glitter and finery was all the means of helping a noble cause. There is no need to enlarge upon the magnifi- cent work which the Infirmary is doing in our midst from day to day. All that is well known and appre- ciated. Funds were never more urgently needed than at the present time, for it has become imperative to undertake an extension of the building at a cost of £15,000. Already the response to the appeal 'itts been most encouraging but it was felt that a fancy dress parade would afford an opportunity to the populace generally, in however humble a way, to contribute their share. The Warrington Cycling Club,ever enthusiastic in the cause of charity, very willingly took the lead in this project and soon enlisted the co-operation of a large band of helpers. Only those who have been brought into touch with the inner workings fully realize how great a debt of gratitude is due to those who gave their time and energies to so carefully arranging every little detail. They felt rewarded, however, In knowing that complete success crowned their efforts, aid that was all the return they desired. Saturday might have been aptly called " Infirmary Saturday," and the.niggard, if he ventured abroad, must have had an unpleasant time. At almost every street corner there was a collecting stand with its red cross and motto : " To aid the suffering." Persuasive ladies did their utmost to wheedle coins from the passers-by, and how well they succeeded was shown by the heavily-laden boxes which were handed over to the Parade Treasurer at the close of the day. Bands of boys and girls were also abroad on the same good erratic], and some contrived to attract the more atten- tion by appearing in fancy costumes. They, too, seemed to reap a good harrPst. However perfect the arrangethents, and however willing the townspeople might be to rally in support of the effort, an event like a cycle parade would, with- out fine weather, be doomed to failure. it was there- fore a matter for distinct congratulation that, although now and then the outlook was threatening, there was not the slightest rain to mar the festival. To while away the time of waiting for the approach of the procession a very happy innovation Sad been hit upon. Warrington people are generally accus- tomed to look upon pierrot performances as attractions reserved for the annual trip to the seaside. Onl Saturday evening, however, they had only to take a stroll along Sankey-street or Bridge-street, as the case might be, and they could enjoy this popular form of entertainment to their hearts content. What added zest and interest to the venture was that the pierrots were all local singers. Their merry songs and quips and cranks kept the audience in fine good humour and when the collecting bags went round the shower of coppers would have almost satisfied the seaside pierrot. The names of the troupe were :—Messrs. A. Grounds, Fred Fieldhouse, J, Willett, S. Hassall, W. A. Clay, G. Gongh, Jame; Emson, P. Hignett, J. Sudlow, and Tom Taylor. Attractive programmes of solos, duets, quartettes and choruses were presented and went with a fine swing. Leaving the streets, which at half-past four were becoming crowded,it was a pleasing change of scene to enter Bank Park where the task of marshalling the processioe and judging the characters was in progress. Round the bandstand were grouped a remarkable variety of figures. There were the weird and grotesque the strikingly original and artistic, while the wealth of colour made a pretty picture. Soon the strains of approaching bands were heard and the nine sections of the parade having been placed in capital order, the signal for the start was given. The waiting multitudes were made aware of the approach of the procession by the appearance of a motor car which traversed the complete route in advance. In it were seated : —Mr. W. P. Itylancls (President of the Parade Committee), Mr. W. H. Bellian (chairman), Mr. G. Bowman (vice-chairmar), Mr. E. Pickavanco (hon. secretary), and.,Mr. E. A. Sanders (assistant hon. secretary). Promptly to time the pageant come into view—and a picturesque one it was. At the head was the band of our local Volunteer battalion discoursing lively music. It was a notable feature of the parade that so many bands were ready to come forward and give their valuable help. They acquitted themselves most creditably and deserved all the praise that was accorded to them. There were, in addition to the Volunteers, the follow- ing bands :—Blue Coat School, Padgate Industrial School, Walker's Brewery, The Concertina Band, League of the Cross League of the Cross Juniors, Salvation Army, Leigh-street Subscription, and The Borough. In quick and almost bewildering succes- sion there came a long line of 'cyclists in costume with gaily decorated machines, while here and there well-devised tableaux arrested the eye of the onlocker‘ And what a medley of characters there was ! Historical persenages in all the glory of court dresses were not many paces from a bold highway- man or a complacent coster, while Chinaman followed clown, and a fierce-looking Indian chief was accom- panied by a sprightly monkey. Then there was many a burst of laughter as grotesquely attired individuals went through all manner, of amusing antics. more

THE OFFICIALS.

Too much praise cannot be accorded to the band of officials who had thrown themselves so whole-heartedly into the work of preparation. They were :—President, Mr. W. Peter Rylands, J.P. ; chairman of committee, Mr. W. H. Bellien ; deputy-chairman, Mr. G. Bowman ; hon. treasurer, Mr. Charles Leigh ; general hon. secretary, Mr. E. Pickavance ; assistant general hon. secretary, Mr. E. A. Sanders ; reporting hon. secretary, Mr. John Blythe. Committee officials :—Finance : Mr. C. H. Wood, (chairman) ; Mr. G. IL Smith (hon. secretary). Prize: Messrs. W. Habberley, (chairman) ; and E. Whitham, (hon. secretary). Entertainments: Messrs. J. L. Davenport (chairman), and F. Fieldhouse (hon. secretary). Cross, of Hoylake, made la pleasing picturd as "Marguerite." Berry of Warrington represented that intrepid high- wayman "Dick Turpin." It would take too long to describe many other notable characters who added to the attractiveness of the display. Princes, cow-boys, cavaliers, Indians, costers—all were to be seen in this

splendidly-varied procession. There were comic per- sonages galore. One ludicrous figure was a gigantic footballer with the Warrington colours bearing the watchword: "Are we down-hearted ? " In this role Mr. Albert White created boundless mirth. His brother Mr. Frank White was dressed in a weird and wonderful style as Porgie "and strode about on a huge pair of stilts collecting coppers from the crowd. Mr. Peter Bradford, of Warrington, was also very conspicuous on stilts 9ft. 81n5. high and by an ingenious arrangement he contrived to wheel a machine. A highly amusing feature was " The broken record "by Mr. J. T. Cooper, Warrington. His machine was an antiquated, dilapidated specimen and bore the legend " From John O'Ciroats to Land's End." Lloyd, of Warrington, clad in the traditional Highland dress and carrying a whisky bottle, represented " Special Scotch." Mr. Samuel Boxers, of Runcorn, supplied with a net and other fishing' tackle went about fishing for coins," his bait being "charity." A curious contrivance was the " 170-horse power motor car " rigged up on cycles by Messrs. Kendrick, of Chester, who appeared to be driving with a determination to win laurels on the racing track. Mr. Dick Scarisbrick, of Warrington, turned out as" A Mad Motorist" and his antics caused many a ripple of laughter. Altogether the turnout of cyclists was a most creditable one, and it is interesting to note that the Runcorn Club secured the award for the largest muster.

OTHER FEATURES.

But these did not exhaust the attractions of the parade. A troupe of lads and lasses from Runcorn (under the direction of Miss Shaw) danced the cake walk with a charming grace and simplicity which won general praise. Then there were the Crewe Royal Morris Dancers who very cleverly went through their popular dance and as they tripped about to the jingling of bells the scene was a bright and picturesque one. The Anamese Straw dancers executed some rather wierd evolutions while brandishing swords, and their " show" was decidedly novel. Some ear-splitting " music "was produced by the Casey's Court Bend,from Penketh, the members of this distinguished combina- tion being clad in all manner of strange costumes, and playing the most unique and fearful "instru- ments.' Three Fire Brigades took part in the parade—Warrington, Messrs. Crosfields', and Wilderspool. Each made a smart display with its decorated engines, but Crosfield's carried oft the prize with their imposing four-horse turnout. A num- ber of boys from People's College who at the sub- sequent concert gave a Fire Brigade sketch rode in uniform on the mr tual engine of the Warrington Brigade. There were several effective tableaux. Messrs. Crosfleld illustrated "Perfection Washing Day," a , number of girls being cheerfullyat work over the dolly- tubs. Cooking methods" behind the times "and " up- to-date" were the subject of Messrs. Richmond's dis- play,the one depicting the hard work and unsatisfactory results under old systems and the her the scene of contentment in the home where modern cooking appli- ances are at the service of the housewife. .Messrs. Pendlebury and Co., showed how easily washing might be done" before dinner" and their tableau was very well designed. The trim yacht" Mephisto "was taken from its native element and conveyed round the town by Messrs. Monks Hall and Co. Mr. Walmslev, an organ grinder, who has been an inmate of the Inflrmare showed his appreciation of the institution by co'lecting on its behalf during the whole of the day. In the procession his organ, drawn bya pony, was surmounted by a cot containing a child whose head was swathed in bandages. A pretty feature of the procession was a car drawn by a pair of Indian goats driven by a little maid. It was but fitting that the "Beautiful Warrington " movement should be reflected in the parade ; a miniature garden, very tastefully arranged, being conveyed on two bicycles. The tableaux was in charge of Messrs. Frank Lynch and Harry Trevor (Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.) The whole of the route was thickly lined with spectators who were loud in their admiration of the display. 'there were 300 collectors and nine collecting lorries so that the public had ample opportunity to help the Infirmary on this occasion.

CONCERT IN THE PARR HALL.

The attendance at the smoking concert held in the Parr Hall on the conclusion of the parade was some- what diacippointing. No doubt the hot weather was responsible foithe bmallness of the patronage bestowed. 'Prior to the concert a very creditable organ recital was given by Mr. IV. Pearce, and was much appreciated. The, concert progratme was a most admirable one. many local artistes taking part. The Warrington Male Voice Choir, under the conductorship a Mr. Hassall, sang the choruses " Song of the Crusaders," "The Village Blacksmith," and "Spartan heroes " with fine taste and expression. Mrs. Arthur Booth's rich voice was heard to distinct advantage in that touching old Scotch air " Annie Laurie, which she gave with exquisite feeling. Miss' Garnet Ball sang " The Carnival" in a tasteful add pleasing manner. Mr. W. H. Ireland was warmly applauded for his interpretation of "Bonny Mary of Argyle," and Mr. J. B. Poole gavea spirited rendering of "The Bedouin love song." Miss Winifred Pigot's cleverly executed xylophone solos proved popular items and Miss Amy Pigot delighted the assembly with the monologue " I and my father-in-law." The Lyric Quartette party (Misses A. Woodland and L. Fairhurst, Messrs. R. W. Cook and P. J. George) submitted a number of selections in their best style," Toothache "—a very amusing item—being particularly acceptable. Messrs. Alf ed Grounds and J. Emson were the humorists of the evening and their songs and sketches were very well received. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac.. ably accompanied. During an interval Mr. W.P.RYLallos briefly thanked the public for their support to the parade and concert. He did not know whether they all appreciated the fact that they were approaching a very interesting period in the history of the Infirmary. in 1010 it would celebrate its centenary. In theyear 1810 a number of gentlemen in the town recognized the great need of an institution which would provide relief for the sick poor, and a dis- pensary was started. That ran for many years, and was the source of a great deal, of good work, but it was found that a mater work was required, and that the scope and field of operations should be much wider. The result was that the Infirmary building was started about the year 1870, and towards the cost of that insti- tution there were some magnificent donations, including one which Warringtonians would always remember with feelings of pride and gratitude—the donation of £lO,OOO by Mr. Hatton. Having dealt with the great need for an enlargement of the Infirmary to meet the growing needs of the town, Mr. Rylands said all were highly gratified at the great and remarkable success which had met the anneals on behalf of the extension fund. The sum of £8,868 had been subscribed, although the fund had scarcely beeq open three months. It was also a source of extreme gratification to the committee,l of which he had the honour to be president, to see so i great and sincere interest taken n the work by all classes- in the town, and on behalf of the committee he tendered to all the heartiest thanks for the handsome way in which they had been supported. (Applause.) He was sure that when the accounts of that day's effort were closed it would be found that £5OO had been realized to add to the extension fund. (Applause.) Having referred to the practical interest evinced by the employers of the town in the extension scheme, he remarked that the employs were all determined to show their appreciation of the Infirmary's work. In addition to the numerous small contributions, the employes of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles had promised £5OO. and the employes of his (Mr. Rylands') firm were going to subscribe £3OO. There VMS a similar move- ment going on in connection with other works in the town. (Applause.) At the conclusion of the concert Mr. Rylands dis- tributed the prizes to the successful competitors. SCIENCE NOTES AND IN ENv. FOR WOMEN AND *OMEN ONLY

PHOSPHORESCENT WOOD AND PEAT.

Decayed wood, and especially peat, has occa- sionally been observed to evolve a faint light, and even some flowers have been remarked to give out brilliant flashes of light during a warm summer's evening. The light, emitted with- out apparent heat, is termed phosphorescence. Various animal substances become luminous before decay has begun, and especially the flesh of certain fish, of which the most remarkable are carp and sole. Herring, lobsters, and crabs, of ten emit a phosphorescent light.

A HORNED HORSE.

A curiosity of the British Museum is the skulk of an English horse shewing a pair of rudi- mentary horns on the forehead. Older specimens are a. portion of a similar skull with the horns and models of the foreheads of two thorough- breds formly over the horns, with no sigi of thickening breds also having horns. The ski extends urn- or hardening. Just what these ho i-like growths signify is a perplexing problem, as none of the ancestral horses, or immediately related species, were horned animals, and if there is no here- ditary influence it is remarkable that so many fruit appendages should appear in the same place.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY DO STARS TWINKLE?

An explanation of the familiar rayed or star- ,ike appearance of the stars when seen by the naked eye has been attempted. It is said that all stars sheW precisely. the same rays, but that in the case of the brighter stars the rays are. plainer and somewhat •longer. It is further re- marked that the rays seen by, the left and right eyes diffir, and that if the head be turned the rays are rotated in a corresponding manner. It is thus concluded that the source of the rays is not in the stars but in the eye itself, the middle of the retina being not perfectly homogeneous in its sensitiveness.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
WASPS AND SUICIDE.

Some time ago a scientist, being curious be see the effect of benzine on a wasp, put some of it under a glass in which a wasp was imprisoned. The wasp immediately shewed signs of great an- noyance and anger, darting, at a piece of paper which had introduced the benzine into his cell. By-and-bye he seems to have given up the un- equal contest in despair, for he lay down on his back, and, bending up his abdomen, planted his sting thrice into his body, and then died. The experimenter, continues the Family Doctor, allowed his scientific interest to overcome his humanity so far as to repeat the experiment with three wasps, only to find that -the other two did likewise. He is, therefore, of opinion that wasps, under desperate circumstances,. commit suicide.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW WHITE "CAST IRON."'

Two parts of aluminium and one part of zinc form an alloy to which has been given the name "slzene." It is equal in strength, says Popular Science Siftings, to good cast iron and superior to it in the matter of elastic limit. It takes $ Inc smooth finish, and does not readily oxidise. The colour is white.. It melts at a low red heat and is very fluid, running freely to the ex- tremities of the mould, and filling small or thin parts. Great care, we are told, must be exercised in melting it, particularly when mixing the two metals, 'in order to preserxo its smooth working qualities. It is somewhat brittle, and hence un- suited to such pieces as require the toughness possessed by brass. The tensile strength is ap- proximately 22,0901 b.. per square inch, and 3•3 is the specific gravity.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
PATIENT'S RECORD TEMPERATURE.

Dr. Heller describes the case of a fifteen-year- old girl, who went through an illness of about a., week's duration, of which the only tangible symptoms of note, in addition to the fever, were moderate swelling of the spleen and a soft heart murmur. The temperature .was remarkable, how- ever, in tat on one occasion it reached 113 deg. Fehr., and` on' several others 1121 Fahr., the upper of which limits has, according to the doctor, never before been reported. In taking the temperature four different thermometers of tested accuracy were used, and the readings' were checked by three other persons in addition to Dr. Heller. All of the manifestations usually' attending high fever were absent in this case. The tongue was moist and not coated, the brain was not affected, and the heart was compare. tively quiet. ,

===== 0002601_19060728_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR DAY SLOWLY LENGTHENING.

For some time Mr. P. H. Cowell, Chief Assist- ant at the Royal Observatory, has been at work on the Lunar Theory, and in his efforts to bring the movements of the mool into harmony with the universal law of gravitafion ho has carefully examined the records of certain well-authenti- cated ancient eclipses to determine the secular acceleration of the moon, a factor in the problem it is impossible to determine by modern observa- tion.. By hypothecating lunar and solar accele- rations of llsec. and 4sec. respectively in a cen- tury, Mr. Cowell established the accuracy of these records, but, unless the earth be moving through a resisting medium, the sun's accelera- tion is contrary to gravitational theory. " As- suming', however, the acceleration to be only apnarent, Mr. Cowell suggests that it is due to tidal friction retarding the earth's rotation, one result of which is that the day is lengthening every century by the two-hundredth part of a. second!

===== 0002601_19060728_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER WONDER OF THE WORLD:

A new wonder has been added to the world's great engineering feats by the completion of the new Croton dam of the New York city water- works system, says the Technical World Mgga- zinc. The Corticll darn—as it is sometimes called because of its site "being a part of the old Cornell farm--in its colossal masonry, in height, thickness, and volume of water confined has no rival. It is larger than the three great dams of Europe combined—the Vyrnwv darn in England, the Furens in France, and the Gileppe in Bel- gium. and while the Assouan dam, controlling the irrigating floods of the Nile, is longer than the Croton, it is only 70ft. in height, as com- pared with the 301 ft. of the latter. The once narrow Croton river now forms a lake 2,400 ft. wide at the dam, and reaching up the valley almost twenty miles, with an extreme width of two miles. The new dam is 2,400 ft. long, 216 ft. thick at the base, and tapers towards its crest, where it is 10ft. across the top of the spillway and 21ft. wide at the top of the main. dam. The water at the dam is 1.60 ft. deep.

TEMPERATURE OF THE SUN.

Many diverse estimates have from time 'fro time been made of the temperature of the 'mu Father Secchi, the distinguished Italian a%creno- mer, placed it at the enormous figure of 10,800,000 deg. Fahr. Other estimates are small in comparison. Thus, Violli made it 3.032d,?g. to 5,432 deg., Pouillet and Soret 2.052 deg. to 3,092 deg., and Wilson 11,732 deg. The data on which the estimates have been made are a little uncertain, and a trifling difference in the neces- sary assumptions makes- an enormous difference in the result_ A more recent and reliable esti- mate is that of Professor Moissan. The distin- guished French chemist brings forward.conside- rations which tend to show that the temperature of the sun is not far different from that of the electric furnace. The temperature which can be obtainet. by means of the voltaic are is estimated at from 6,ooodeg. to 7,000 deg., and there is no known substance which cannot be liquefied and volatilised by this. Now, tile substances which occur in the sun are for the most part the same as those of the earth, and in the sun they exist in the state of vapour. Professor Moissan, then, considers that the temperature of the sun can• not exceed that at which its substances distill, and this would be the heat. of the electric fur- nace, viz., 6,ooodeg. to 7,ooodeg. Fahr. It must be remeznberea, however, that if the pressure under which theme frapotus exist in the sun is much greater than those um:ter wrucn toe correspond- ing substances have been vapourised in the elec- tric furnaces, then a correspondingly highs; titimperature might bo required._

TEM kXAMINER.

JULY 28th 1906.

It is a risky thing to write

THE TWO about Russia nowadays, for REVOLUTIONS, the whole situation may have been changed, and by world-shaking events, before what is written appears in print on the breakfast table. Events in Russia, as has been remarked, are closely follow- ing the precedent of the French Revolution. One newspaper observes " That the parellel lines may not be produced indefinitely must be earnestly hoped, even by those who believe most firmly in the righteousness of the popular cause and the pressing need of the substitution of the constitutional for the autocratic system." The words are obviously based on a misapprehension, not only of what is now taking place in Russia,but of what took place in France towards the end of the eighteenth centry. Comparatively recent, but very complete and conclusive, research has proved that the events of the French Revolution were,like the report of Mark Twain's death, grossly exagger- ated. The documents on which the history of that great event has been based were written on one side by those who desired to exaggerate the blood-guiltiness of the Revolutionaries, and on the other by those who felt the need of magnifying the crimes and the cruelties of the regime which was overthrown. What occurred in France has already been outdone in Russia. The imagination does not shrink from turther horrors because they leaay approach the French scale—for that scale has already been excelled—but because they may, and probably wll, transcend all human experience.

The events of the French Revolution were dramatic. They had that conciseness and

impressiveness which comes of being confined within a comparatively narrow space. They wore a bloodthirsty aspect, without doubt ; but from first to last, exclusive of lieromiox's wars which sprung out of them, they did not involve anything like the large sacrifice of life and property which has already taken place iu Russia since Red Sun- day. Probably more lives are sacrificed every year by violence in Macedonia than were destroyed by the Meloch of the Revolution. There were not a dozen prisoners in the Bastile when it was pulled down; but the Russian prisons are full to overflowing, and chiefly full of political prisoners. The war in the Vend6e was nothing as compared to what is taking place in certain of the Russian provinces, where the peasants are burning out the landowners and reducmg themselves to helplessness by destroying at the same time the crops and the seeds which might ue reproduced .:1 The general condition of Russia is anarchy quali- " I led by organised massacre. The arranging of " pogroms " seems to be the only kind of organise- Con that still remains. , Those who ivish well to Russia and less well to autocratic systems of government will desire that, the parallel between the Russian and the French Revolutions shall not be prolonged to the extent , of the final" whiff of grapeshat." The long agony ; ir of the Russian people has deserved something , better than e tinction by wholesale slaughter. Yet so far as can be foreseen, the triumph will rest in the long run with the rifle bullet, the sword, the machine gun, and the knout. The alternative prospect is that Russian liberties may be preserved by the financial necessities of the country. Tyranny is at the end of its resources. It has been assisted so much already by the wealth of other countries that it is alleged by those who have the best means of knowing that we are on the verge of a tremendous financial crisis which will involve all Europe, but particularly France and England. In the general ruin, both the autocracy and the bureaucracy of Russia may go down. It is sad to think that this is almost the only ground of hope for the Russian people.

There was much that was A Boom IN TRADE. extremely interesting in the speech which Mr. Cecil a,.. F. Parr, the chairman of Parr's Bank, Limited, c delivered to the shareholders at the meeting of ' 1, the company at Warrington on Thursday. We , believe we are right in saying that Mr. Parr's ! remarks on these occasions are always looked for- ward to with pleasure, for he does not content himself with merely reviewing the position and prospects of the bank but takes a wide survey of financial and commercial matters generally. His speech on Thursday revealed the gratifying ' fact that during the past half year there have been "good times." To those who have been apt to take a pessimistic view 'of our commercial prosperity, it should be re-assuring to know that trade is booming. Lancashire's great staple industry—cotton—is nourishing, many mills working at full time and having sufficient orders on hand to keep them going for many months to come. Warringtoe people will be more closely concerned with the iron and steel trades and here again Mr. Parr was able to report most favourably. As an indication of the boom which is being experienced he pinted out that the output for Middlesborough alone for the last six months came to over 700,000 tons, aS against 460,300 tons for the previous half year. ,Those people who are incessantly groaning about " dumping" and foreign comp3tition will I perhaps be surprised to hear that LO present both Germany and America are buyilo iron and steel from us. Mr. Parr alluded/"ii undisguised satisfaction to influences whieuf u-E, they became more pronounced, must be for t`,',., permanent benefit of our trade. One was tl-w wave of temperance at present sweeping over the country and the other the increased readiness evidenced by masters and men to settle disputes by arbitration instead of resorting kto the "ruinous and barbarous methods" of strikes and lockouts. No one, we imagine, Will feel disposed to quarrel with Mr. Parr's view as to these great forward movements and we can only express the fervent hope that both temperance and arbitration will make more rapid strides. Without entering upon a consideration of the interesting hut intricate'subject of whether there is sufficient gold in the world to go round—on which Mr. Parr spoke at some length—we would commend the speech to the thoughtful perusal of our readers.

As a result of the Royal MOTOR CAR LAW. Commission on Motor Cars. some very important and interesting recommendations are made, though the cars are neither to be taxed out of existence nor forbidden to raise dust. From the point of view of the majority the most valuable and strik- ing proposal is that taxes on motor vehicles shall be increased, and the revenue from all such taxes shall be allocated to the different local authorities , for the improvement of the roads. MotoristS I themselves will welcome this retommendation, for there will be less objection to even an increased tax when it is recollected that the money is to ha ..° spent on the roads. Nothing is said about road- , construction— which was perhaps outside the prov- ince of the Commission—but it is to be hoped that the road makers will of their own accord,or under the guidance of some central authority, adopt a different method of construction. The ordinary macadam road is almost as unsuited to the triffic of the day as the stone pavements of 100 years ago. A better " binding " material than mud and grit is required if any reduction of the dust nuisance is to be effected. There is lik4y to be more diversity of opinion in regard to the pro- ' posed abolition of the general speed limit of 2,0' miles an hour and the substitution of a twelve mile speed limit at dangerous spots. Motorists will probably hail the idea with delight, and if they and their drivers were all of one type—the - best—there could not be much objection from other road-users. But the road-hog is still prevalent and if he has a nice stretch of road before him, free from cross roads, hills and the like, he is certain to let his car " zip " at any speed uP, to a mile a minute. The objection to this the effect on cyclists and horse-drivers. Th : are hundreds of cyclists—both male and female who at present cannot suppress a tremor nervousness when a car comes past from behi with a rush and a roar at the legal speed-limi But what will be their feelings when the speed-. limit is abolished and cars can indulge in express speed on the open road ? In all probability we . should hear much oftener of shocking fatalities like the one a week or two ago near Tarporleyr when a nervous girl-cyclist wobbled straight into a car through sheer fright. The speed questiori ' is one which must be very carefully considered from many points of view so long as we have 50 many classes of traffic on the roads. The other numerous recummendations of the Commissioo are such as will find general favour, and an altera' tion of the law on such lines is probable. --- A correspondent takes the smoke nuisance another column. He something done to lessen it, and we hope be wh• 0 be successful. It would be better if he were ill th, Town Council and then he could second t' . efforts of Alderman Bennett in the same direct.io Warrington is one of those conservative-00tdie-r, political sense' of course—places where Me ici methods are slow to catch on. As Mr. Gtrla3e3 of his letter points out, there is no qnestion of fact as to the immense possibilities in the watillow smoke prevention, and if manufacturers ar,ed to move in the matter, the spur shoui. applied by the local authority whice responsible for the health of the Sons Ltd. munity. Messrs. Crosaeld and is

BLACK SMOKE.

MEM

, ' have set an excellent example locally, and proved ' that there is no need to poison the air with black 'smoke from furnace chimneys. A large wire-works nothing like one hundred miles from Messrs. Oros- , fields' front door sets an example as bad as their's is good. Most of the large works in the town follow snit—or soot—and of smoke prevention ' there is very little. Not long ago a watch used to , be kept on Warrington chimneys, and occasionally some black offender wonld be haled before the bench—consisting often of brother offanders—and fined. The fine was small in comparison with the volume of smoke, and proved little of a deterrent. Now there is not even an occasional prosecution. and Warrington's tall chimneys belch forth 4, soot and blackness as often and as long as . they like. Possibly the Act relating to the ' emission of black smoke does require amending, but the education of the manufacturer in his office , and the stoker at his furnace are more necessary. Until we get a healthy pablic opinion on the ' question there will oever be a healthy atmosphere in manufacturing towns. "Beautiful Warriugton" .( offers reason for hope, and indeed we have more faith in efforts on these lines than in all the Acts of ,Parliament ever drafted. But S,noky Warrington will remain Smoky Warrington for some time yet.

Now that everyone is think- HOLIDAY WAys jog of 'holidays and of how AND MEANS. best to spend them, one's thoughts are apt to go ;,. hack to th 3 many plans which have been thought of and acted on in former seasons, and to wonder what new thing in this line will be tried this year. One of the most novel ideas was that of two 4`o, 'Nloting ladies. They took a furnished cottage, and it was noticed that several days after moving ria they were never seen to enter or leave the house. The neighbours became alarmed. The door was locked, and on the police breaking into the house the damsels were found in bed, where they intended to stay for the rest of the time. ,We do not think, however, that this form of holiday is likely to become popular, although it could no doubt be recommended on the score of cheapness. Other cheap holidays such as camping- out will no doubt attract a far greater number, while walking tours and boating will also claim their adherents. The simple-life idea is certainly popular—at least its theory is—but in prac- tice it either means " roughing it," which iew people really like, or else spending one's time in a quite out-of-the-way village where there are not only no niggers but no danc- ing, no fashions, and little chance of meeting and making the acqu tintance of other holiday-makers This is very good, no doubt, for some invalids, hut .moat people would find it too terribly dull. After all, though they complain of the overcrowding and 00 on, most people will find that on the whole the much•depised " fashionable resort " has plints of its own which outweigh the advent iges of more new-fangled and generally unsatisfactory ways of spending a holiday. Besides, whatev-ir we may , say, English people are too conservative to make any radical changes, even in their methods of obtaining " a change of scene."

-- Whatever else may be said, THE DREA.DNOUGHT. the Board of Admiralty , have certainly made up their minds to a policy, and having done so are Prepared to carry it out. The memorandum issued by the Board dealing with the details of the Dreadnought proves that. Toe sea-fights in the recent Far Eastern war proved the value of Powerful long-range armament to the battleship and demonstrated, too, that under conditions such as prevailed between the 'Japanese and Russian 2eets secondary armament for battleships became . not only useless but an unnecessary burden. S) in the new ships it was decided that there should be , no intermediate armament, but that the ship should carry ten 12in. guns so placed as to give ,-, , he maximum of fire on the broadside and a very large proportion of fire ahead or astern. The deci large ion was conrageops, for it struck out a new path i l'or the British naval policy. Yet it was the prac- tical conclusion of an experience like that of the battle off Port Arthur two years ago, when Togo's battleships by the power and accuracy of their it::o3:24rin-fire reduced the Russian Fleet to r "neY. It will be interesting to see whether Other nations will follow our example, Certainly Japan oußht when she provides herself with further battleshiv,,.
