NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman is regarded through- 1/4, 'ti '.. nt the Parliamentary circles of Europe as one of the 4 sturdiest champions of liberty and democracy.—. 'Glasgow Herald." * * * • It was a rather singular circumstance that not a single competitor in Saturday's Cycle Parade endeavoured to turn the tinned meat scandals to good account. This was surely a case of neglecting to improve the shining hour. * * * Sir John Brunner, M.P., with his usual generosity has offered to nay the expense of sending the children at Dutton Workhouse to the seaside for a day's holiday. Needless to say, the offer was accepted '' with thanks" by the Runcorn Roard of Guardians.

* * Our readers will be interested to learn that Mr. 'Prancis Neilson, who delivered such stirring and eloquent speeches in Warrington just before the e setion campaign, has been responsible for the crganizing of the Liberal meetings in the Cockerineuth division which average about eighteen a day. * * It has been pretty generally suggested that the exclu- sion of the Bank Quay district from the route of the 'cycle parade was to some extent responsible for the the falling off in the collection. We should not wonder if that were the case for even "gorgeous dis- plays of tinsel and paper" will not draw some people up to town, whereas they can be successfully appealed \ to if the procession passes their door. * * * _

The St. Helens Health Committee has decided. to

appoint a lady doctor wLo will assist the Medical Officer of Health and attend specially to School Children. A salary of £l6O is offered, rising in two annual instalments to £2OO. If every big town goes in for medical superintendence in this thorough fashion, the problem of what to do with our girls Will be solved. They will all want to be lady doctors under municipal authorities if they can thus earn .C. 3 to £4 a week.

* * * Tree-planting at Stockton Heath was a much vexed question a short time ago, but the weather has been too hot lately to argue about such things. The fact still remains that many of the trees are a nuisance and a danger, and it is some consolation to learn that the Rural Council admits " there are trees which are somewhat in the way." Ti e time is.not opportune for removing them, but the .ifrdent tree-planter must not imagine the matter has then forgotten. * * * Trains and bicycles are very convenient modes of locomotion but they certainly come as an" ill wind" to the boot manufacturer. In his speech to the share- holders of Parr's Bank, Ltd., on Thursday, Mr. Cecil P. Parr attributed the decline in the Leicester boot trade in great measure to the saving of shoe leather ,effected by trains and bicycles. As he remarked, this Is a curious instance of how the social habits of a People may materially affect the demand for even so Primitive and universal a material as shoe leather. 5 * * Admiral Jones Parry thinks there is room for the activity of the Beautiful Warrington Society in the neighbourhood of Grappenhall Brook, "the most , nasavoury spot he had ever seen." Quite possibly there is scope for some such labour here, but it ought to Come from Grappenhall and Thelwall, and the Eallant admiral might very properly lead the way. fact that Warrington people drink the milk of e cows that drink from the brook savours a ltttle of „the house that Jack built," but it is no reason why "eY should do other people's work.

When Mr. Meyer, president of the Baptist Union attended a meeting at Brighton, sornesne reminded him that he had preached there fourteen years ago. Mr. Meyer said that he would give a penny to any one who remembered the text. A man held up his hand, and repeated the text, and amid enthusiasm Mr. Meyer handed him the penny

* * * "When do you call a man drunk?" asked Super- intendent Ennion at Runcorn of. a woman named Jones, who had come to prove her son was sober. "When he cannot pull his shoes off and get upstairs," was the reply, amid loud laughter. This is not a bad standard to set up, and seems superior to the chalk- line method. There can be no doubt of a man's drunkenness when in the state described.

* * * Atelegrat in the daily press states that Miss Rosa Symonds has just completed a cycle ride from London to Land's End, to John o' Groat's and back to L mdon. The distance covered was 1,815 miles in 14 days. This almost incredible performance works out at 130 miles a day—a feat of endurance at which most men would crumple up. If Miss Symonds spent her fort- night's holiday in this fashion she must be a glutton for work. Personally we can see no sense in this kind of cycling unless there is a substantial reward at the end of it. "Fourteen days hard" would be a dreamy spell in paradise in comparison. * * *

The suffragette has come- to stay. To that event we may as well Make up our minds ; her strident yell Is heard amongst us every day. Poor man at present is at bay Endeavouring in vain to quell The suffragette. In time, no doubt, she'll get her way, (When that will be no man can tell) But—pray divulge not where I dwell— Woman does not deserve, I say, The suffrage yet !

—" Punch."

* * * A certain Burnley church magazine contains a report of a mother's picnic, which, after mentioning that the mothers at dinner consumed soup, fish, beef, lamb, ducklings, plum puddings, and a host of other edibles, continues :—" Then an adjournment was made to an adjoining field, and enjoyment of another kind appeared. •We frolicked like young lambs. We sang, we rolled one another down slopes and, oh! how we laughed! and why not? We live hard lives beneath grey skies, and amid moist surroundings; and this was 'our day out ' ; and we meant to enjoy it ; and Ivo did."

* * * In a churchyard less than ten miles from Manchester (writes a Church correspondent to the " Manchester Guardian ") I last week found a lofty marble monu- ment with an inscription in gilt letters stating that it was erected by the members of a licensed victuallers' association to the memory of their secretary, "in recognition of the valuable services rendered to the trade by his energy and perseverance during the long period he served their interests." Whether the vicar of the parish was in 1835 consulted as to this inscrip- tion is of some interest. Its legality might certainly have been contested at the time, but I can imagine Sir, Wilfrid Lawson's ghost haunting that spot. * * *

According to a report on -experiments in the pro vention of dust on Lancashire roads, presented by the County Survevor,the use of palliatives would cost £4O pe r mile. There are 472 miles of macadamised main roads and 057 miles of macadamised secondary roads in the county and the cost of treating these alone Would amount to 5.57, 160 per year. Under the circumstances we can see little use in continuing the experiments. A. remedy of this kind is out of the question because of the expense. The County authori- ties had better set to work to find a cheap, binding road-material to take the place of the present duet- producing macadam. A permanent cure, and not a palliative, is required. * * * The motorist need no longer look for "the man in blue" in Dorsetshire. He has vanished and in his stead appears the man in grey, Owing to the dust nuisance it has been decided on the recommendation of the Chief Constable of Dorset that sergeants and policemen shall in future be provided with grey instead of blue uniforms in the summer. This is a striking innovation, and if the example becomes at all generally followed we may expect an outcry from the fraternity who find it necessary to keep an eye on the " bobby." If the colour of the latter changes in every county, the rogue and the vagabond and the law breaking motorist will have to carry a sort of identification catalogue like unto the booklet of motor numbers which the police find useful.

*4„ * We are surprised to learn that such a mild and peaceful gentleman as the Rector of Warrington has read that very fanciful story "The Invasion of England in 1910," and has moreover been much interested therein. Indeed so much was the Rector impressed with the feebleness of the national defence as pour- trayed by the novelist that he made it the subject of a warning word in his annual sermon to th.) Volunteers at Church on Sunday morning. They were to remem- ber that " God had given them not only this country, but possessions in all quarters of the globe—in America, Africa, Asia, with the whole of Australia, . . . . and they should be prepared for defence." Some of these gifts of God were really filched by man, but that by the way. The clerical mind is apt to get rather out of its depth when it ventures into the realm of military necessity. Most of the colonies are well able to look after themselves, and in any case our volunteers are intended solely for home defence.

Band of Hope Picnic. The members of the Hill Cliffe and Stockton Heath Band of Hope Society held their annual picnic on Thursday. Assembling at 7.30 the party, which num- bered 120, left Stockton Heath by 'bus for Bank Quay station where a special train awaited them. Warring- ton was left at eight o'clock and Morecambe, the place selected for the outing,was reached an hour and a half later. The day, which was gloriously fine, was pleasantly spent and after tea at the Crescent hotel the return journey was commenced, Warrington being reached by 9.30.

LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDEK TIIE CLOCK. BRAD GE-STR.EET.—Advt. A Neglectful Mother. At 'Warrington, on Thursday, Sarah Ann Davison, Cartwright-street was charged with neglecting her two children. Inspector Cos stated that the case was adjourned last month to see if defendant would improve. He had visited the house on several occasions since, but she had made no effort towards improving the conditions of the home or the children. The elothing'of the children was almost in rags, the defendant being too lazy to sew buttons on. House- hold articles and clothing had been pawned for money for drink. The magistrates committed her to goal for a month.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Cost of Vaccination.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesdav when there were present :—Mr. A. R. Norman (presiding), Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. J. Douglas, P. Collins, S. Taylor, S. Woodward, T. Wright, C. E. Linaker, J. Balshaw, .T. Hallows, C. Rfflance, J. Spruce, EL Eyes, H. Mavvdslev, Geo. Egerton Warburton, W. Weir, T. Antwis, Bretherton. J. Whitley, T. Guest, H. M. Davies, G. Pilling, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), J. Cuanah (master), T. Cook, W. R. de Prez and J. Pearson (relieving officers.)

THE COST OF VACCINATION. The Finance Committee reported the payment for out relief of £7O to Mr. C00k,Z26 to Mr. de Prsz and £25 to Mr. Pearson. The accounts for vaccination were Dr. McDougle £3l 2s. 6d., Dr. Carruthers £6 is. 6d., Dr. Barton £l2 3s. 3d., Dr. Bower £2l 4s. 6d., Mr. Cook, £lO Bs. Id., de Prez £6 155., and Pearson £3 ISs. 10d. The Chairman : It is something to be thankful for that the cost is decreasing.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL ---- Stockton Heath Tree Planting.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District ,Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday when there were present :—Mr. George Pilling (presiding), Admiral Jones Parry,Messrs. G. Egerton Warburton,J. H. Davies, S. Woodwand, T. Wright, C. E. Linaker, J. Bolshaw, J. Hallows, C. Roylance, J. Spruce,H. Eyes, H. Mawdsley, W. Weir, T. Antwis, E. Bretherton,J. Whitley, T. Guest, J. H. Davies, G. F. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Houg4 (assistant clerk), J. Swinton (surveyor), J. Farrington (inspector) and Dr. Adams (Medical officer). SEWAGE WORKS AT STOCKTON HEATH..

The tender of Mr. T. Stringer, Warrington, for the construction of sewage works in Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, at £ll2 19s. 6d. was accepted.

CLAIM FOR COMPENSATION.. The Clerk said that Mr. ft. Roughsedge, Appleton Thorn, wrote on behalf of his father-in-law. Mr. John Baton, who was injured by the fall of a wall Nvhilst working for the Council about three years ago and asked for compensation. He had been under Dr. Bowes more or less ever since the accident and all be had received had been 10s. from Mr. Swinton. It was decided to adjourn the application pending a report from Mr. Swinton. NORTHWIGH LIGHT RAILWAY.

The Clerk reported that the Light Railway Com- missioners bad approved the Warrington and Northwich Light Railways (extension of time). 'Order 1906.

THE SURVEYORS' SALARIES. At a meeting of the Highways Committee applications for advances ir, salary by the surveyors, were considered and it was resolved that in view of the salaries paid for similar duties in other districts the Council be recom- mended to make no alteration.

The recommendation was approved. TREE PLAIN'TING AT STOCKTON REATH.

. - The committee also considered the question of tree planting at Stockton Heath and recommended that the matter be fully considered and decided upon before the 1 season arrived for the removal of trees. The committee were fully alive to the fact that there were trees in Stockton Heath which were somewhat in the way. The present time was not, however, opportune for moving trees and they would have ample time to send a com- mittee to the district to decide which trees were to be removed: There was no doubt some must be moved if the convenience of the public was to be properly considered.

SURVEYORS ESTIMATES. The committee also considered the question of the excess of expenditure over the estimates of the surveyors. If the surveyors were as competent as they hoped they were they ought to be able to forecast to a given percentage the cost of road improvements and repairs and the committee hoped that next year they would be able to point to a better state of things than at that time.

Mr. J. H. Davies pointed out that in the Budworth district the estimate for repairs had been exceeded by £5 Bs. per mile or 17 per cent. He took it that if a business manager could not estimate to within 17 per cent. there would very soon be a vacancy. Mr. C. E. Linaker said that under certain conditions he did not consider the excess in the Budworth district to be extraordinary. A great deal depended upon the weather as to whether the estimates were exceeded or not. • The reports were approved. 'MP. CONDITION OF ORAPPENHALL BROOK. Admiral Jones-Parry, commenting on'a report pre- sonted by the Grappenhall Brook Committee, said the spot was the most unsavory he had ever seen. It puzzled him how people could be allowed to build house in places which were not sanitary. If people who were looking after The beautifying of Warrington went and put their noses to the Grapperdaall Brook they would find there was plenty of work to do, especially as the cows which supplied milk to Warrington drank the water out or the brook. Mr. Hallows said that where the cows drank was not the worst part.

OUTTIREAK OF SCARLET FEVER. Dr. Adams reported that during the month eight cases of scarlet fever had occured in the district, five of these being located in Stockton Heath.

ST. HELENS WOMAN'S DEATH. Hemmed Inquest.

GRAVE ALLEGATIONS AGAINST A MIDWIFE.

Verdict of "Manslaughter."

On Monday, at St. Helens Town Hall, the county coroner (Mr. S. Brighouse) resumed the inquest on the body of a married woman named Ruth Dinah Gibson, forty-one, of 97, bole Bank-street, who died at the St. Helens Providence Hospital on Saturday night, the 20th ult.

The inquiry, it will be remembered, was adjourned from Monday week in order that Mrs. Annie Taylor, a midwife who was in attendance upon the deceased woman during her confinement at the beginning of June, might be legally represented. Mr. J. HaslamFox appeared yesterday morning on her behalf, and Detective-Inspector Dunne watched the case on behalf of the police. James Gibson, a collier, and widower of the deceased, stated that his wife was unexpectedly seized with ill- ness on the night of June 9.Mrs.Taylor was called in and when he saw her he thought she showed signs of drink, but he could not describe her as being drunk. After he had left Mrs. Taylor with the deceased for about half an hour he was called into the room and told that twins bad been born. A fortnight later Dr. O'Keeffe was called in, and he attended the deceased until she was removed to the hospital. Mrs. Taylor had twice previ- ously attended the deceased Cross-examined by Mr. Haslam Fox, witness said he was quite content to leave his wife in Mrs. 'faylor's charge, as he did not believe that she had had much drink.

The Coroner remarked that the allegation which the police made against Mrs. Taylor was that she was culpably negligent in the performance of her duty, and that in consequence the deceased woman came by her death, The case was one in which she ought to have called in a medical man, but had failed to do so. Ellen Jones, a ten-year-old daughter of the deceased, who was sent for Mrs. Taylor on the night in question, asked as to the sobriety of the nurse, stated that Mrs. Taylor stumbled on the road to the house, and didn't " favver " (seem) sober. • Gibson, recalled at the request of Mr. Fox, was asked whether Mrs. Taylor had advised the calling in of the doctor. He replied that so far as he was aware no such suggestion had been made. Margaret Wainwright,a neighbour,said she was in the Gibsons' house on the night of June 9, and had Mrs. Taylor under observation for about half an hour, and thought she was suffering from the effects of drink. Dr. P. J. O'Keeffe deposed that when he was called in Ito attend Mrs. Gibson on June 2 he found her in a low land exhausted condition, and two days later he notified Dr. Buchan, the medical officer of health, of the case. On the 20th the woman was removed to the Providence Hospital, and died there the following day. On the 2nd inst. he and Dr. Buchan made a post-mortem examina- tion and they were agreed that death was caused by blood poisoning due to incomplete delivery. Undoubt- edly the case had beea one in which the attendance of a medical practitioner was required. The proper pre- cautions had not been taken to prevent blood poison- lug. I Dr. John J. Buchan, the St. Helens medical officer, confirmed the testimony of Dr. O'Keeffe as to the con- clusions formed on the results of the post-mortem examination. Mrs. Taylor had been registered since 1905. The rules of the Central Midwives' Board required that wbere the death of the mother of the child occurred before the attendance of a medical practitioner the facts should be notified to the local supervising authority, but that had not been done in this case. Mrs. Taylor was illiterate, and could not write her own name. _

The Coroner remarked that all women who at the , time of the passing of the Midwives Act could prove I that they had been practising for a ye,tr as midwives and had good characters were admitted to the register. Mr. Fox asked whether women with long experience I were not as efficient as those who had passed examina- tions. The Coroner : Well, we have passed the days of Mrs. Garnp and Mrs. Harris, I hope. In answer to a juryman, Dr. Buchan said that Mrs. j Taylor had apparently attempted an operation which. according to the rules, ought to have been left to a t medical man. The probability was that the woman's life would have been saved if medical skill had been requisitioned. Inspector Sisson stated that on Saturday, June 20tb, he took Mrs. Taylor to the Provicence Hospital in I order that she might be present when the dying deposi, tions of Mrs. Gib-son were taken. On the way to the hospital Mrs. Taylor insisted on making a statement, t denying that she had done any harm or exceeded her duty in any respect. When they arrived at the hospital Mrs. Gibson was unable to make any statement. Evidence as to the sobriety of Mrs. Taylor on the occasion in question was given by a woman named Ann Leather, who lived in the same house, add three other I witnesses. Dr. J. S. Fox stated that even with the most expert t medical skill delivery might be incomplete, and he did I not think there was was any satisfactory evidence to I show that the midwife had done any wrong. I The jury returned a verdict of " .1-anslaughter." : Death, they said, was the result of an illegal act per- formed by Mrs. Taylor,but as to whether she was drunk at the time of the operation, the evidence was not sufil- cient to show. IShe was committed to the Assizes on the coroner's , warrant on a charge of manslaughter. MRS. TAYLOR CONINIITTED FOR, TRIAL.

At the St. Helens Police Court, on Tuesday, Mrs. Taylor, a midwife, of Park-road, was brought up charged with manslaughter. I She had to be assisted into the dock by two con- I stables, and seemed to be utterly prostrate, appearing , to feel her position very acutely. ! The magistrates on the bench were Messrs. W. J. Thomson, 13. B. Glover, and J. W. APTear. lhe prisoner , was represented by Mr. J. Haslam Fox. The Chief Constable briefly related the facts of the case, and added that he would call precisely the same evidence that was given before the Coroner, and if they thought there was a prima-facie case, he would ask , them to commit the prisoner for trial at the assizes. Evidence was then called similar to that which had been called in the Coroner's Court, nod prisoner was I committed to the Assizes, bail being refused.

THE PROPOSED NEW SCHOOL

AT PENKETH.

SANKEY PROTESTS AGAINST CONTRIBUTING. Parish Meeting. The decision of the Lancashire County Council to erect a new school at Penketh has occasioned some alarm in the adjoining township of Great Sankey as it was thought not unlikely that Sankey would be included among the parishes that would have to contribute towards the cost. This, it is contended, would be a hardship, as Sankey has for many years maintained a school of its own which provides ample accommodation for the children of that township. In order that the ratepayers, residents, and property owners might have an opportunity of expressing their reeling in the matter a Parish Meeting was held in the Council Schools, Liverpool-road, on Monday evening. There was a large attendance, Mr. George Thompson (chairman of the Parish Council) presiding. The other members of the Parish Council present were :—Messrs. W. Taylor, A. Birch, H. Faircloughatml•W. Furnessovith Joseph Smith (clerk). The Chairman said he thought he was the first person who got to hear of the proposal to include Sankey in the list of townships to be rated for the schoel at Penketh, and he heard it with a great deal of surprise. He took immediate steps to call the Parish Council together to take the matter into their serious considera- tion and three or four meetings were held. The action decided upon included writing to the Board of Educa- tion, the local Education Committee at Earlestown, aud the County Council. The Parish Council had certainly done all that it was possible for anyone to do and, he thought, not without some very good results. Fr.= what they knew that night, their action appeared to have been looked apon in a very satisfactory light and the ratepayers had reason to congratulate themselves that the Parish Council had acted in the way they had done. The Clerk and himself received some information of a pleasing character but the Clerk thought it would be better to have something definite to lay before the parish meeting. In reply to a telegram with reference to a meeting of the local Educatioa Committee held at Earlestown the other day' the clerk to that committee (Mr. Clarke) wired:—" Local Committee only recom- j mend Penketh and Cuerdley contribute cost of school." I Therefore as far as the local Committee were concerned, they did not approve of Sankey being saddled with any ' cost whatever, and he (Mr. Thompson) thought that I was a very great step in advance. He, however, con- sidered it was advisable that they should approach the County Council and not let the matter drop. If any of those present had had any thought that the Penketh people were trying to use any influence to bring Sankey ; into the arrangement, they must disabuse their minds of that altogether. Penketh had had nothing whatever to do with it. For twenty years Sankey had been paying for its own school, and it would still have to contribute for eight or nine years. It would therefore be a great injustice if ' the township' were saddled with any of the cost of another school. (Hear, hear). Mr. Thompson suggested the appoint- ment of a committee consisting of the Parish Council and several ratepayers, to take any further action that might be considered necessary. Mr. NV. D. Jeans said the question was one which particularly affected farmers beause they had to bear a great proportion of the rates. If. he continued, the ratepayers objected to the school the proper authority to approach was the Board of Education. They were not to take any representations from Earlestown that they did not mean this or that, because if they let three months elapse they could not approach the Board of Education. Whatever might be the ultimate result they ought to present a petition to the Board of Educa- tion direct. He had drawn up a petition which he thought was suitable for presentation. Mr. Jeans then read the petition which pointed out that Sankey had ample accommodation for the elementary education of all the children of the township, the number which the school would accommodate being 342. The total cost of the school was about V. 750, a portion of which Still remained to be paid by the ratepayers of Great Sankey by annual instalments. The ratepayers of Bold, Cuerdley and Penketh,did not contribute any part of the cost of the building, although the Sankey School had been and was still used by children of the township of Penketh and of the district now comprised in the borough of War- rington. The petitioners submitted that, having regard to the fact of the rates of Sankey being already burdened with the cost of providing a school, and to the future economy of the rates, no portion of the 1 expense of providing the proposed new school ought to be borne by Great Sankey, as the school was wholly unnecessary as far as that township was concerned, and the charging of any portion of the expense on Sankey would render the education rate intolerably heavy. Continu- ing, Mr. Jeans said that if the matter went on, the County Council had power to charge such a proportion as they thought fit, not being less than half or mare than three quarters of the expenditure incurred in pro- viding the school on the parishes served by the school There was no reason whatever why Sankey, having. schools of its own, should be put to the expense of I providiras a school which would be practically for the benefit of Penketh, Bold and Cuerdley. The clerk read a letter which the Parish Council had already addressed to the Board of Education stating Great Sankey's position in the matter. The Board replied that it was for the County Council and not the Board of Education to decide what parishes were served by any school provided by them. If it was desired to apnea] to the Board against the school it was necessary that the appeal, unless it was made by the managers of an existing school or by the local Education Authority, should be signed by not less than ten ratepayers. Mr. Jeans said they should follow the technical course laid down by the Act and petition the Board of Educa- tion. He moved that the petition he had read be signed and forwarded to the Board. _ .

Mr. Birch seconded and said the Sankey school pro- vided accommodation for 100 more children than the township at present sent to school. It would be a great hardship on Sankey,after providing a school of its own, to be saddled with any additional outlay. The motion was unanimously carried and it was also decided to forward a copy of the petition to the County Council. Many of the ratepayers present signed the petition. It was further agreed that the Parish Council together with Messrs. Jeans, Kilshaw, Cook, Dixon, Tongs, and Unsworth, should form a committee with power to incur any expense which might be necessary in dealing further with the matter. THE "MOTOR NUISANCE." DRASTIC MEASURES NEEDED. Mr. Tongs coruplained of the disturbance caused by traction engines passing along the highway during the night. . The Chairman remarked that the motor business was a nuisance from beginning to end to farmers, house- holders, landlords and everybody else ; and something must be done to put it down. The question tas being seriously taken up by different councils in the country, and he believed some drastic measures would be taken.

Mr. Tonge said it was not so much' of m-o-to-rs- that he complained as a heavy traction engines which passed in the night and prevented one from getting rest. The Chairman : I don't know of anything that appeals to everybody like the rooter nuisance. We are all more or less affected by it. I say something must be done and will be done before long- to put down the nuisance.

SANKEY TiBOOK. WHO IS TO BLAME FOR THE NUISANCE?

The Chairman said it would be remembered that some time ago Messrs. Furness and Jefferson were deputed by the Parish Council to inquire into the matter of Sankey Brook pollution and they presented a long and very valuable report. He thought the ratepayers would bear him out when he said that there was a great improvement in the condition of the brook for some time but at present the brook was becoming a very great nuisance. He thought the ratepayers of Sankey had a perfect right to take up the matter and try to find' out who was responsible for the nuisance. He had an idea himself and he thought if they appointed another deputation to go into the matter and take the pains that Messrs. Jefferson and Furness did, it would be a very good thing. There was a remedy for the nuisance And there was no doubt that if it could only be found out who polluted the brook they could be punished. In moving a vote of thanks to the- Chairinan, Mr. Furness said the Sankey Brook question came up as a hardy annual. He thought the local authority was much to blame for the objectionable fumes. When the Parish Council approached tho District Council, that authority at once authorized the Surveyor to make pro- vision for dealing with the offensive matter which they a District Council turned into the brook. He thought as a member of the District Council, the Chairman should keep " pegging " at that body and insist off them cleansing their own output. The District Council could do nothing until they stopped their own objectionable matter being turned into the brook. Continuing, Mr. Furness said Mr. Thompson had done all he possibly could, and he had been well backed up by the Parish Council and ratepayers, in trying to combat the effort which the County Council were making to foist on the parish part of the responsibility for the cost of the Penketh school. He ventured to prophesy that the action they had taken was of such a character that it was a foregone conclusion that Sankey would not be drawn into the arrangement. Mr. Tonge seconded and the vote was heartily accorded. M 4. THOMPSON TO RETIRE. The Chairman said he had been amongst them nearly eighteen years and he had always tried to do what he could for the benefit of Great Sankey. Possibly that would be the last meeting of ratepayers he should have the pleasure of addressing. It was his intention to retire from the position of District Councillor and Guardian. When they elected him to the offices he made up his mind that he would attend to the duties and do all he possibly could for the ratepayers. That he had done. In the future, however, he we's afraid he could not spare the time, even if his health would permit, to attend to the work properly and therefore he thought it only right to give them due notice that when his term of office expired in March he would not offer himself for re-election.

ISSUES GREAT AND CURIOUS.

Salt Union and Brunner Mond in a Big Law Suit.

Considerable interest is taken throughout the Cheshire salt region by reason of the action commenced this i week n the King's Bench Division between the Salt Union, Ltd., and Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Co., the famous chemical manufacturers.l A host of wiinesses, including many scientists and geologists, have been summoned on both sides, and there has been quite an exodus from the district of persons subpsenaed to give evidence. The issues involved are great and curious. The Salt Union claim that the-, have sustained a loss of £38,800 through subsidence, &c., caused by Messrs. Brunner. Mond and Co., pumping brine from plaintiff's mine. Messrs. Brunner, Aloud, and Co. own a mine (formerly in the Thompson family) adj tcent to the Salt Union mine, and the defendants, while denying the cause of action, allege that they are entitled to pump all the brine they can obtain in their own mine. They also plead the Statute of Limitations. For all subsidence caused by brine pumping within , the Northwich district compensation is paid by a levy upon the pumpers. Threepence per thousand gallons is the maximum tax allowed under the Act. '

Domestic salt and nearly all chemicals are made from raw brine, and previous decisions have been on the lines of the law as to underground water. Consequently the great action between the biggest representatives of salt on the one hand and chemicals on the other is regarded 2S a vital importance.

DROWNING FATALITY AT LATCHBRD.

Wail,

===== 0002601_19060728_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
Child Falls into the Bridgewater Canal.

Another sad drowning fatality occurred at Latchford on Thursday afternoon. At about 2.45 a girl named Margaret Stirrup, aged seven years, d iughtcr of Charles Stirrup, painter, 400, KnutsfOrd-road, was playing on the bank of the Bridgewater Canal along with two companions when she fell into the water. Before help was forthcoming she was drowned. Later the body was recovered by Tram Driver Warburton.

MOTORIST ATTACK.ED BY BEES. Mr. Chandos Elletson, of Porrox Hall, Knott End, was motoring towards Lancaster on WeduesdAy after- noon. when the car ran into a swarm of bees. The chauffeur, being stung, took his hands from the steering wheel in order to knock the bees from his face, the result being that the car swerved upon the footpath and collided with a lamp post. The car was damaged, but the occupants escaped injury.

Mr Halliwell, cashier of the Chadderton Spinning Company, Oldham, was leaving a local bank on Thurs- day morning with £4OO in cash in a handbag, when he was attacked with an iron bar, and robbed of his bag. The a,sailaut escaped. J. Wolin has returned to Dover in readiness for his next attempt to swim the Channel, which will probably take place on Monday. It appears that the trouble which developed during his last swim was not a strain, but sciatica.

Mr. For:ahem moved that the resignation be accepted and that the Local Government Board be asked to issue an order empowering the Guardians to appoint a resident meeical officer in accordance with resolutions already passed ; also that the House Committee should consider the matter end bring it before the Board for its final ricciqion. The ASSISTE.Nr CLERK said he advised that the House roiernitme ought to take the matter into con- sideration but he did no+. see how the Board could at that meeting' which was only en interim meeting, pass a resolution like Mr. Forshaw suggested. The Local Government Board required notice to be sent to each Guardian. Mr. UOLLEY se.!onded the motion, contending that it was perfectly justified, and what was suggested did not entail any executive action involvingaexpenditure. If it did, then notice would have to be given, but the main principles had already been accepted by the Board and what Mr. Forshaw proposed was of a formul character. It was not really necessary to pass a resolution accepting the resignation. With regard to the tone of Dr. Braide's somewhat unfor- tunate communication, he ventured to make the suggestion that the Guardians had nothing to blame themselves for in regard to the matter. Their share of the negotiations had been carried out with a single eye to the welfare of the sick poor of the institu- tion and any personalities which had been imported into the 'discussion bad not come from the side of the maioritv of the members of the House Committee or of the Board. That ought to be stated as plainly and frankly as possible. IWithout embarking on a discussion of the nature of the grants in aid which werepaid to , the Guardians in relief of local taxation, the suggestion , made by Dr. Braide that the Guardians were making a profit of £2O a year out of him through grants was altogether uncalled for and misleading. The grants in aid were not given for the remuneration of I relieving officers but for the relief of local ratepayers.' One did not want to be hard on Dr. Braide. Personally I he had tried to make ft as clear es possible that he had no animus whatever against the doctor; on the contrary he had tried to be friendly to him. Dr. Braide had I taken up an undignified position. Now at last he was retreating from it and by a little flourish of trumpets was , trying to make his retreat look as much like a triumphal I march as possible. It was a retreat, nevertheless. The AsB (STANT CLERK said that at the time anoca-, tions of grants were made under the Government ' echeme, Dr. Spinks was medical officer and he received £lOO a year. The grant remained unaltered. ' Mr. HICKMAN emphatically protested against the statement that there was any personal feeling against Dr. Braide in the matter. The committee spend hours and hours in discutaing it in order to arrive at a proper end fair basis upon which they could treat Dr. Braide. He was very sorry the doctor had hintea at any personal feeling in his letter. He (Mr. Hickman) was sure they were all sorry the doctor had met, with an accident. He thought that if there had been any personal feeling it had come from the doctor more than any member of the House Committee. ° Mr. DAVIES : I think the least said in this matter the soonest mended. Mr.FonsHAw's motion accepting the resignation and communicating with the Local Government Board for permission to fill the vacancy by thp appointment of it resident medical officer was carried.

PUBLIC BKSENIT.-UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt.

Militia Training.

The men of the— 3rd South Lancashire Regiment (Militia) to the number of 450 assembled at Orford Barracks on Monday morning, and early afternoon and journeyed to Fleetwood the same afternoon for their four weeks' annual training. When the men arrived at the Barracks each had the option of having either a quantity of bread and cheese and ale, or a hot meal and temperance drinks, over fifty per cent preferring the hot meal and temperance drinks. The battalion paraded at three o'clock, and proceeded to Bank Quay Station under the command of C)lonel Hall, D. 5.0., and headed by the band, and entrained at four oclock. At Fleetwood they will join the 150 recruits of the battalion who have been in camp mice July 9th.: Horse Sale at Walton Hall.

A sale of pedigree hackneys and ponies, including five stallions, the property of Sir Gilbert Greenall Bart., took place on Tuesday afternoon in the beauti- ful grounds surrounding Walton Hall. Prior to the sale, a large company were entertained to luncheon, Mr. Woodcock, of Grappenhall, catering. The cata- logue consisted of 57 animals, out of which only three horses failed to find purchasers. The total realized for the fifty-four was 3,019 guineas an average of £5B 14s. for each horse. The highest prices realized were : —TerringOn Glamour, Mr. Kerr, 72 guineas ; Terrington Josephus, Mr. Kerr, 100 guineas; Terring- ton Victory, Mr. Kerr, 120 guineas ; Tissington Hall- mark, Mr. ,Kerr, 110 guineas.; Tissington Context,Mr. H. Frank, York 90 guineas ; Tissington Kopje, Mr. Harrop, Oldham, 87 guineas ;Terrington Divinity, Mr. Smith, Birmingham, 100 gaineas ; Terrington Virago, Terrington Vera, Mr. F. S. Pearce, New York, 350 guineas ; Lord Drewton, 11. Mi. Besrnaerts, 90 guineas.

PUBLIC BKCEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEIT BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Local Athletic Successes. At the Salford Corporation gala on Monday night at the Blackfriars-road Baths, keen competition took place in a 50 yards ladies open amateur handi- cap, for which 29 entries were received. Miss D. Thompson, of Salford, with 26 sec. start, obtained first position, Miss If. Barlow, of Warrington (20see.), being second. Miss Barlow is the daughter of Pro- fessor Barlow, superintendent of the Baths, Leigh- street. Her performance is very smart, especially considering the fact that she is only 12 years of age, and under her father's tuition she should turn out to be a very successful swimmer.- -At the fourth annual Regatta and Swimming Gala of the Runcorn Boating Club, held on the Weaver Canal at Rocksavage, on Saturday, R. H. Alien, of Warrington, carried off the second prize in the 150 yards open race (time 21secs ) In the 75 yards race A. H. Fawcett, Stockton Heath, gained the third place. In the open race for the same distance the third prize went to T. S. Pemberton of Warrington. At the Salford Harriers' meeting at Belle Vile, on Saturday, W. Price, of Warrington, was third in the two miles walking race.

An Extraordinary Story. A Blackburn deserter named Joseph Arnold has had a remarkable experience. Late in 1904 be becamel a victim to the allurements of the South Lancashire Regiment stationed at Warrington, and he was " well and truly" attested at Damen in the name of Forrest. The recruiting officer saw the man on the train at the talon on his way to the regiment's beadqaarters. At Blackburn, however, " Forrest " deserted and almost immediately afterwards he again went to Darwen and, according to his story enlisted in the same regiment, and with the same authorities as before, this time in the name of " Thomas Hepe." Once more be deserted after a short period of service, STILL LEADING, the "HERALD" KITCHEN RANGES having, in .the meantime, parted with one set of and " PEVERIL " FIRE GRATES Sole Agent.—J. GARLAND, 51, 53 and 55, Bridge-st.—Advt. Hearts of Oak Benefit Society. The first half-yearly meeting of the Warrington Electoral Association of the Hearts of Oak Benefit Society, was held in St. Paul's Infants school on Mon- day evening. There was a fair attendance and in the absence of the President and Vice-President, Bro. Bailey was voted to the chair. —A communikation was received from the Warrington District Nursing Associ- ation asking the society for assistance. Bro. Binks said he thought it would be detrimental to do any- thing more at present than to send their sympathy to 4he Nursing Association. The society was a new one, and perhaps at some future date they would be able to send financial assistance. A proposal that the society should appoint a representative on the Board of Management of the Nursing Association,was left in the hands of the General Committae.--Bro. J. T. Rogers, of Widnes,atten led as delegate and gave a report on the opening of the Heart § of Oak new offices in London.— There are about 500 Hearts of Oak members in the Warrington district, and the Association has been formed in order to band them together. Up to the present about 80 have joined, and have appointed as chief officers Bros. T. Rigby, president ; R. Tanner, vice-president ; G. Jowett, treasurer ; and A. C. Binks, secretary. About a month ago it was resolved to form a Medical Aid Society in connection with the Association, and already 34 members have been enrolled.•

INQUEST AT RUNCORN.

On Monday Mr. R. Dobson, coroner, held an inquest on the body of John White, aged fifty-four, master of the fishing smack Excel, who was drowned in the Mersey on Saturday. Deceased was reputed to be a good swimmer, and his nephew said he was subject to fits.— Thomas Green, Norfolk-street, who was on shore, said he saw deceased walk to the stern, throw up his arms, and fall backwards into the river, and be never rose to the service again.—An open verdict of "Found drowned'. was returned.

Mr DEAR ChrrLDREN, In the hat summer days / have no doubt all sny young readers long for some nice cooling and , refreshing drink. I wonder whether you have ever tasted the sparkling Herb Beer which is , made from Mason's Extract of Herbs. From , personal experience, I can assure you it is "ever so good," and, for both children and adults, is much more health-giving than many onemically-flavoured beverages which are so largely used at this season, and which are posi- tively harmful to young people. The other day I paid a visit to Messrs. Newball and Mason's splendid factory at, Nottingham, andl before I came away I purchased 100 sixpenny bottles of "Extract of Herbs," for the "Chil- dren's Corner." I am going to distribute them free of charge, and I will send one, post free, to the first 100 members of the Children's Corner League who send a postcard or letter ask- ing for same. Please don't enclose any stamps— just say you would like to have a bottle, and if you are among the first, 100 you will receive it well packed and post paid. The prize this week goes to Nellie Roberts; her tale, lam sure, is most interesting. I see you all like the idea of making toilet tidies for our next Competition. I, therefore, propose that all the girls shall make me one, and send it by the 27th of August. This will give you plenty of time. You can make these tidies just as you like, and any shape you please, and to the three prettiest I receive, I shall award very nice prizes. With all good wishes, from

UNCLE NED.

THE LITTLE BOY AND.THE STARS. You little twinkling stars that shine Above my head so high, If I had but a pair of wings, I'd join you in the sky. I am not happy lying here, With neither book nor toy, For I am sent to bed because I've been a naughty boy. If you will listen, little stars, I'll tell you all I did; I only said I would not do The thing that I was bid. I'm six years old this very day, And I can write and read; And not to have my own way yet IFS very hard indeed. I do not know how old you are, Or whether you can speak; But you may twinkle all night long, And play at hide and seek. If I were with you, little stens, How merrily we'd roll Across the skies, and through the clouds, And round about the Pole. The moon, that once was round and full, Is now a silver boat; We'd launch it off that silver cloud, And, then—how we should float. Does anybody say, "Be still," When you would dance or play? Does anybody hinder you When you would have your way?" Oh, tell /me, little stars, for much I 'wonder why you go The whole night long, from East to West, So patiently and slow. "We have a Father, little child, Who guides us on our way; We never question when He speaks, We listen, and obey." From your loving Niece, JINNI E CUMMINS.

HARRY PAYS:

(Concluded.)

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a piece of poetry, which I hope you will like. It is my favourite piece. I was fourteen on the 13th Qf June. This piece is called PAPA'S LETTER,

I was sitting in the study, Writing letters, when I heard, "Please, dear mamma, auntie told 1110 Mamma mustn't be 'asturbed. But Fee tired of the kitty, Wants some uvver fing to do, Witin' letters, is 00, mamma, Tan't I wits a letter too?" "Not now, darling, mamma's busy; Run and play with kitty now." "No, no! mamma, me wits letters, Tan, if oo will show me how." But the eager face was clouded, As I slowly shook my head, Till I said, "I'll make a letter Of you, darling boy, instead." So I parted backtthe tresses! From his forehead, broad and white, And a stamp, in sport, I pasted 'Mid the waves of golden light; And I said, "Now, little letter,

Run away, and bear good news." ,And I smiled, as down the staircase Pattered loud the little shces, Leaving me, the darling hastened Down to auntie in his glee: "Mamma sent me for a letter; I'se a letter, auntie, see l" No one heard the little patter, As once more he climbed the stair, Reached hie little cap and tippet, Standing on the entry chair; No one heard the street door open, No one saw the golden hair As it floated o'er his shoulders, On the crisp October air. Down the street the baby hastened, Till he reached the office door:

"Fee a letter, Mr, Postman; Is dere room for any more? 'Tause iss letter doing to papa: Papa lives with God, oo know. Mamma sent me for s letter; Does oo fink that I tan go?" But the clerk, in wonder, answered,

• "Not to-day, my little man." " Tink I'll find a nuvver office, 'Tause I must go if I tan." Fain the clerk would have detained him, put the pleading face was gone, And the little feet were hurrying, By the busy crowd swept on. Suddenly the crowd was parted, People fled from left to right, As a pair of maddened homes Just that moment dashed in eight. "No one caw the baby figure, No one saw the golden hair, Till a voice of frightened sweetness Rang out on the autumn air. 'Twas too late; a moment only Stood that beauteous vision there— --4* Then the little form lay lifeless, Covered o'er with golden hair. Reverently they raised Iny darling; Brushed away the curls of gold, Saw the stamp upon his forehead, Growing now so icy cold. Not a mark the face disfigured, Showing where the hoof had trod; But the little life was ended— Papa's letter was with God. r From your loving Niece, NELLIE ROBERTI.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am writing you some poetry this week, entitled : TRY AGAIN.

'Tis a lesson you should heed, try, try, try,

again; If at first you don't succeed, try, try, try again., If you find your task is hard, try again; Time Will brineyour reward, try, try again. All that other folk can do Why, with patience, may not you? Ever keep this rule in view—TßY AGAIN. I am also sending you a story called:—

TRY._ / There was once a good little dwarf named Try, who was so powerful that he overcame everything that he attempted; and yet was eo small that people laughed when they were told of his wondrous doing,s, but the tiny man was F. 0 kind at heart, and loved so much to serve +hose who were less able than himself, that he would go and beg of those who knew him better, to plead for him, that he might be allowed to help them out of their troubles, and when once he had made them happy by his roble deeds, they no longer despised him, or drove him away with sneers, hut loved him as their best friend. Try acted on the maxim "Each for all," and when each seeks the good of all in a "Society," the happiness of each is *increased by the "Society." Yet, the only return this good dwarf sought for all his services was when they knew any who wanted a, helping hand, they would say a good word in his favour, and com- mend them to TRY.—From your loving nephew, Enikizsr HelmoLD, 1.5, Flood-street, Pudley. .

THE ROBBERS' CAVE. (Continued from last week.)

The men entered the room and searched it, but did not discover the cowering children. There was a staircase at the end of the zoom, down which the men descended, leaving one at the top as sentry. They waited, listening, till it was very late, and' the men began to think of going to bed. In, a few/hours they did so, but did not got to sleep for some time, for they lay awake talking. The children heard all they said, though the men did nof know • it, or they would not have said what they did. Jack and Florrie listened intently. They heard the men falking about a secret panel, which was in a rock. The children determined to find the secret of the rocky wall after the men had gone to sleep. At length, by their heavy breathing, the children knew that they had fallen asleep. They then cautiously came out from their hiding-place, and ap- proached the rocky wall at the upper end cf the room, as they thought it would be the right one. They felt about a long time, hut without success. They were just about to give it up in despair, when Jack found a square piece of cod, painted just the colour of the rock, which fitted in so el]eactly that it would have taken someone with very good eyesight to have noticed it.4)With the help of Jack's penknife, he managed to prise it out. This done, they found a small button right at the back which they immediately pressed. This caused 'a great mass of rock to move slowly, until there was a liole big enough to allow a man to get through. They did not warate a moment in getting illat *ugh, I can *satire yes. yam BY THE CLUBMAN. The Neglect of Brakes.

Notwithstanding The many warnings which have appeared in the press on the importance of 1112ti11.- taining the efficiency of brakes, a tabulated list a reported cycling accidents during the month of June, circulated by one of the brake manufacturing firms, shows that the risks attendant on the negleot of brakes have not been exaggerated. Out of 13/ accidents, 20 of which resulted fatally, the majority were due to- this cause. Not only should brake blocks be renewed when worn, but care should be taken to ensure that the stopping power of both brakes is maintained. As soon as any feebleness is detected, the slight adjustment re- quired to bring the brake shoes nearer to the rim should be made, especially where hilly ground is bina nag.,ti.t.o4l

Entering krance with a Bicycle.

Cyclists contemplating a French tour should note that any member of a club affiliated to the National Cyclists Union may obtain from Mr. S. R. Noble, 27, Chancery Lane, W.C., on payment of Is. 6d., a pass securing free admission for his cycle into France. All that then remains 'to be done on landing at a French port is to pass the machine and luggage through the Customs, and to obtain a perrais tic eit`eulatzon, which costs 6d., is available for three months, and renders unnecessary the registration of the cycle. At Dieppe oi• Calais any one can enter with a part-worn bicycle who is obviously a bona fide tourist and wears a cycling Club badge. At other ports, however, unless the cyclist is prepared to deposit the duty on his machine, he must be armed with a membership ticket of the C.T.C., N.C.U., or Touring Club de France.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR:,

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
The Smoke Nuisance.

Sir,—lt is nearly ten years since I called attention in the columns of one of your contemporaries to the smoke nuisance, and the points which I emphasised were : 1. That in the opinion of experts and competent authorities, a large percentage of smoke can be abated without unduly harassing or interfering with trade; 2. That in most cases both master and man gain by it because the increased cost of labour and plant is more than counterbalanced by the decreased cost of fuel. This has since been proved in our own town by Messrs. Crosfield who recently made the remarkable statement that they were saving £23,000 per annum by improve- ments to save fuel and prevent smoke. Although this will probably be regarded as an exceptional case, it shows what can be done by up-to-date scientific methods. But the result of smoke abatement as regards profit or loss to capital and labour is only considered by Parliament as a secondary matter. The question, a burning question truly, is one primarily affecting the public health, and it is now over 30 years since the Public Health Act, conferred the neces- sary powers upon Sanitary and Local Authori- ties. How is it that some towns and cities are doing their best with the Act and with very satis:. factory results whilst others are doing nothing. Can anyone tell us what Warrington has done or is doing in the matter ? I pointed out in 1896 that the time limit allowed at Glasgow for the emission of black smoke was three minutes and in Liverpool it was in four minutes andlwhen these limits were exceeded a summoes was invariably issued. In one year 330 of such summons were issued in Liverpool and in 513 cases the prosecu- tion was successful. With such facts and figures as these before us, will anyone venture to say there is any reasonable excuse for the dense volumes of smoke which are daily pouring over Warrington? Let any of your readers who are interested in this question, watch some of the chimneys and they will find that the Liver- pool and Glasgow limits are multiplied by three or four in scores of places every day. If they have any doubts about smoke being injurious to health let them watch the prevailing winds and ask a few questions of the people living in the localities most affected. They will not be long in realising the evil, but in some cases the sufferers will say" We have to put up with it, and it can't be helped, it wont do for me to say anything about it." Do the members of Health Committees and Sanitary Authorities ever reflect that morally and indirectly they are responsible for hundreds of deaths and thousands of shortened lives if they 'neglect to use the powers which were given to them by the Public Health Act ? Is it not part of their duty to piotect those who cannot protect themselves ? If your readers have any doubt about the magnitude of the evil and the opinions of competent authorities permit me to quote one or two published utterances. At a Glasgow Congress, with reference to the Smoke Nuisance, Pro- fessor Ramsay said "It directly incites disease, and "it has now been shown to be a positive danger to health." Another high authority. Dr. Russell, senior medical officer of health for Glasgow says. " Now-a- 4.ays the undulations of the mortality depend more upon our artificial local climate than upon the rise and fall of zyrnotic epidemics." Dr. John Haddon speaking at Manchester said about smoke "I cannot stay now to reckon up all the evil that has been wrought by this grievous compound whose presenee in the air of our towns is the more to be regretted because it has been proved over and over again by practical men to be quite unnecessary and easily prevented. Floating over our great centres of industry likes dense cloud, it shuts off the genial light of the sun, and deprives the inhabitants of one great stimulus to vital action. It also passes with the air into their tender delicate lungs and by its constant irritation leads to the large class of diseases of the respiratory organs, that form so large an item in the Registrar General's returns from this part of the country." Is it necessary to say any more? Will anyone seriously contend that this great evil should be tolerated any longer? In concluding let me briefly allude to an , excuse which a few sanitary Authorities have made for doing nothing. They say the wording of the Act is vague or indefinite, and opinions vary about the colour of smoke, Of course they do, but it 19 not a borta-fide excuse for ignoring the spirit and intention of the Act. Parliament expects local authorities to put in some common sense between the lines, at all events, that is what many authorities do. If we wait for a perfect Act of Parliament, we shall wait till Doomsday. But if these flaws in the Act am o really the cause of the evil being unchecked for 30 years, our Member for Warring- ton would be doing a great public service not only to Warrington, but to other towns if he would introduce a Bill for amending the Act. I hope, at all events, he will give the matter some consideration.—Yours, &c., W. CHRIMES,

C 79, Knutsford-road, Latchford.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEER ENCAMPMENT. To-day's Encampment.

To-day (Saturday) the Ist V.B. P.W.V. leave Warrington for their annual camp training. The battalion is 1,126 strong and it is eNpected that almost 1,000 of the men will spend the fortnight under canvas on Windmill-hill, Salisbury Plain. A ' fatigue party, under the command of Captain and Quartermaster A. F. G. Potter and thirty-five men departed for Salisbury Plain at midnight on Tuesday, to prepare the camp for the accommodation of the battalion to-clay. The Mounted Infantry Company, Cyclists, cooks, officers' grooms, as detailed, up to 100 non-commissioned officers and men, will proceed by the 10.30 a.m. train. Officers' chargers and private horses will be sent by this train. This party will parade at the Drill Hall at 9.15 a.m., and will be medically inspected before leaving. The remainder of the battalion will parade at 6.15 pm, and will proceed by two trains to Salisbury Plain. The officers on the first train will be Lieut.-Colonel J. A. Edelsten, Sergeant-Major G. H. Ingram. Major and Adjutant A. F. Bundock, Regimental Staff, A, B, C, D, Companies ; and on the second train Major J. D. Fair- clough and Colour-Sergeant Instructors Allardice and Cleaver. The men will parade in marching order with forage

caps. Major J. D. Fakelough will be responsible for the cleanliness of the camp ground of the left-half battalion. Major B. Fairolough will be similarly responsible for the right-half battalion. The annual inspection will take place this year while the battalion is encamped,at a date to be fixed hereafter. Owing to the distance to be travelled, only those men who can attend for the full period can be present at inspection. Every member must be present unless he has received written leave of absence from the Commanding Officer for some sufficient cause. Conse- quently every member unable to go to camp must obtain this leave of absence. A Church parade will be held on Sunday at 11.30.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEERS AT CHURCH.

The annual church parade in connection with the Battalion took place on Sunday, when the men, to the number of about 350, including the m;:unted infantry, cyclists, &c., attended the morning service at the Parish church. The officers present were :—Lientenant-Colonel Edelston (commanding), Major J. D. Fairclough, Major B. Fairclough, Major Appleton, Captain Smith,Roberts, and Owen, Lieutenants Ridgway, Monks, Lapage,Picton Bournplarey and Woods, Captain and Quartermaster Potter, Major and Adjutant Bundock, with Sergeant- Major Ingram. Many of the soldiers stationed at Orford Barracks were also present, and the church was well filled. The sermon was preached by the Rector, who took for his text the words, God raised up unto them David to be their King, to whom also He gave testimony, and said I have found David, the son of Jesse, a man after mine own heart, which shall fulfil all my will." During the course of his address he said he had been reading a very interesting book entitled "The Invasion of England in 1910." and the point for their notice was that Lord Roberts, the head of the Army, commended the book to them as worthy of consideration. Lord Roberts told them that was just what would take place] if such an invasion was attempted, and it, therefore, behoved them all to be prepared. God had given them not only this country, but possessions in all quarters of the globe—in America Africa. Asia, with the whole of Australia, as well as America, and they should be pre. pared for a defence. The service was a hearty one, and apparently much enjoyed by all. Before the congregation dispersed the National Anthem was sung. The accompaniments to the hymns were supplied by the Volunteer band.

GRAMMAR SCHOOL SPEECH

DAY.

PROFESSOR TOUT AND THE VALUE OF EDUCATION.

The summer holidays in connection with the 1 the Warrington Grammar School are preceded by a highly enjoyable function known as Speech Day. Thursday witnessed the holding of this annual gathering and in every way the proceedings were highly success- ful. The students at the school appear in the role of entertainers and right well do they acquit themselves. As in the past the meeting was held under the chair- manship of the popular headmaster, the Rev. E. J. Willcocks, and the large room, which was gaily decor- ated, was crowded with the parents and friends of the boys.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORK OF THE YEAR.

The Rev. E. J. WILLCOCKS' who had a hearty reception said:—" It is usual for me to summarize briefly the progress of our work, and to record the successes obtained during the year by present and past members of the school. The school is examined and reported on annually by examiners appointed by the Oxford University Local Examinatien Delegates and most of the boys in the two upper forms enter for the certificates granted by the University. I believe that last year's list was in a certain sense a record one. Out of 14 candidates who obtained certificates 9 were I placed in the Honours Division, and four were specially distinguished in various subjects. Allcandidates passed in Religious Knowledge, English and Arithmetic. All but one in Latin and French, all but two in Mathe- matics and Greek, and all but three in History. Five boys offered Higher Mathematics, and all five were successful. Two boys are excused the Examination known at Oxford as Responsions in stated subjects, and three boys—under the new regulations—are exempt from the Matriculation Examination of the University of London. The certificate results for this year are of course not yet published, but the Examiner has sent in his report, and it will perhaps interest you to hear his general remarks:—" I can confidently speak well of the work which is being done at the school, and the Head Master is to be congratulated in having so competent a staff of assistant masters associated with him. The curriculum is a wide one, but the general results of the examination show that the teaching is thoroughly good. It is a marked and a most successful feature of the system that Latin is taught to all the boys, and lam sure that much of the general excellence of the results is due to this fact. Not that other subjects are in any way neglected. Iu Mathematics a high standard is reached, but it is probably a direct result of this teaching of Latin that the French is so much better than I have generally found it, and that more boys than might be expected in a school of this character take up Greek so successfully. The written answers too of the Scripture and History papers show an accuracy of information and clearness of expression which are most commendable. So far as I could see from a few days' residence in the school the general tone and discipline are excellent and I can only hope that when the alterations to the school buildings now in process are completed it will be easier to carry on the work of the school and to develope it upon those lines w hich have slready proved,theruselves so successful." The past year has been one of m.uch anxiety. The new scheme for the government of the school is drafted and may be expected to come into operation shortly. The chief alterations are in the clauses which refer to the j constitution of the governing body—which in future will consist of eleven representative and four co-opta- tive governors—and next, to the abolition of the school divisions. i.e. Upper and Lower School, which will give more freedom and elasticity in the internal organization of the school both with respect to fees and curriculum. The school is now thoroughly efficient and meets all the requirements of the Board of Education both education- ally and structurally.ln addition to the enlargements we made last year we have now added—chiefly by adapting the School House—two class-rooms, workshop, dining hall, cloak-roomsete--and are able to accommodate about 130 boys. The number of boys in the school has been steadily rising during the past two years and this term 105 boys have been present. A four years' course, approved by the Board of Education, has been planned for boys from 12 to 16 years of age, and Government grant in aid is received from the Board. The successes and appointments of past and present boys during the year include :—J. Bamforth. matriculated at Liverpool University in the First Division and also won the Gee Scholarship of £25 per annum for two years. Also passed the First Examination in both parts for the degree of M. 8., Ch. B. R. Kennon has been awarded on entrance after open competitionl a Tate Scholarship of 33 per annum for three years at Liverpool University. J. A. Jones ,1 has graduated BA. London University. M. .k Burgess, R. Kennon and T. B. Weaver have shownl stfacient merit in the Senior Oxford Local Examina- tions to be excused from the Matriculation Examination of the University of London. J. Wilkinson has passed the Preliminary Examination of the Incorporated Societe of Accountants and Auditors. C. T. Cooke, the Final Examination with Honours of the Incorporated Law Society. E. Gandy, the Intermediate Examination for the degree of M.B. and Ch.B. Victoria University. D. Skelton, the Intermediate Examination of the Incor- porated Law Society. K. W. D. Macrae has graduated M. 8., Ch.B. Edinburgh University. A. F. Corney, Wadham College, Oxford, has graduated B.A. in the English Literature Final Honours Schools. We have , also to congratulate Mr. R. E. Kenyon, M.Sc., F.C.S., , on his appointment as secretary to the Technical , Instruction Committee at Sutton Coldfleld, and NIT.. , Seger Owen, F.R.1.8.A. on his election as a Fellow of the Royal Inetitute of thitish Architects. (Applause). PROFESSOR TOUT AND THE VALUE OF

===== 0002601_19060728_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
E DIJCATIO N.

Firofessor Totrr, of the Manchester University,having handed the prizes to the successfnl students, said he was told that their school was irt an interesting state of transition. He hoped that when the period of change was over, and they had a new scheme, that the school would be on a more solid basis than before and that they would be able to enjoy more fully the benefits of the recent legislation which had put into the hands of Town and County Councils the ultimate responsibility for the control of education of all kinds within their respective areas. He was glad to see that for two suc- cessive years boys from the Grammar School had obtained scholarships to the Liverpool University, but he thought that the school could with advantage send more boys to the universities than was the case. He hoped the headmaster did not suffer from what he had heard complained of a good deal in other quarters —a tendency for boys to be withdrawn at an untimely early age, (Hear, hear.) He was sure it was in the best interest of the boys, even if they had to make a living in business, not to cut short their education at a time when the fruits were really beginning to ripen. (Hear, hear.) A capacity to deal with affairs, and to have broad views and an intelligent appreciation of facts was much more likely to result from a good all-round education in a good school, than if the boy began at an early age and acquired a business experience by sticking stamps on wrappers and addressing envelopes and other not very broadening prozesses which small boys in offices were told to do. (Applause). The Grammar School seemed to be conducted on wise lines. He was very glad in going round the school to see that with all the conces- sions to modernity in the shape of workshops, labora- tories, &c.—all very excellent so long as too much time was not spent in any one—that the older educational traditions were not forgotten in the school. He was particularly pleased to hear that all the boys learnt Latin, and that German was an alternative subject to Greek as a classic language, and, that with the conces- sion to antiquity, they recognized that there were other languages and literature as well as those which traditions said should he so largely taught in English schools. He had to confess that the old traditional classic education was as soul destroying as was the modern trend to teach boys nothing but mathematics and , chemistry. He beli6ved boys should have the same teaching in the literary and historical as in the scisotifios expression of human activity and thought. The best cirriculum was en all round one and he hoped that when the moment of excitement was past the school would drift into a position which would neither take the form of the old traditional system nor the more utilitarian form of the new, for the simple reason than if they wanted to have even in the narrowest and most commercial sense, an education which paid best it was not that which taught only things which would be of use in after life, but which sharpened the wits of the boys in order that they might grapple with any emergencies which might arise. He hoped that when they started upon their new career with the premises re-modelled and brought up to date they would I have, as they ought to have, a great future. (Applause.) He also hopod that when the stream of fructifying guineas poured in from the Town Council and pious benefactors all would not be taken up in bricks and mortar and plant and material. They laid of course to spend a good deal in providing apparatus and rooms and there was no doubt the money was well spent, but he felt that for every £lOO laid out in that way there ought to be at least an equal sum to equip the human agents who were of infinitely greater importance in / building up the real school, though he feared that some people, especially seine official people, did not always I realize that. (Applause.) He felt that it was far better to conduct a school in a series of tin huts or wicker cottages or even on the road-side when it did not rain, without apparatus and plant at all, so long as Ithe school was taught by the right sort of people. (Hear, hear.) They could only get the right sort of people by paying them properly, secur- ing the future for them and recognizing their work. England did not properly recognize the schoolmaster—he was afraid England did not really ' much believe in education—and there was no country !, where the master, the ordinary assistant master was thought less of than in England. We boasted of our I radicalism yet in foreign parts the school masters and notably the assistant masters were much more recognised much more appreciated and were in a better status in ; society than were the same class in England. He would I like to impress upon the governing body of that school, ; and they would probably agree with him, and upon the

===== 0002601_19060728_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
