The Directors reported that the position and pros- pects of the business continued to be in every way satisfactory. Including £100,953 13s. Bd. brought for- ward at 31st December, 1905, the net balance of profits for the half-year, after providing for all bad and doubt- ful debts, and rebating the current bills at 5 per cent was £307,092 14s. 3d. From this sum the Directors recommended :—The payment of a half-year's dividend at the rate of 19 per cent per annum payable, free of income tax, on Ist August, £162,307 10s. ; the transfer to bank premises account, £15,000 ; the transfer to Officers' Pension and Provident Fund, £10,000; leaving balance to he carried to next account, ..'119,785 4s. 3d. THE CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH. THE IMPROVEMENT OF TRADE. TheCHAfitmAN said he ended his address to the share- holders in January with the remark that they had had a good tirne,and that the bank was in a stroll.- position , and ready to take advantage of the good times that I apparently were coming. Those good times did come I and he thought the shareholders would see from the balance sheet that the company duly took advantage of them. If anything the hank WAS in a stronger position now than it was in January. The average bank rate for the six months had been £3 18s., as against £2 13s. 9d. for the previous six months, so that they were able to supply all the legitimate wants of their customers with a satisfactory profit to themselves. He neeil not come to tell a Lancashire audience that TRADE HAD BEEN GOOD. Taking Warrington for instance, which was a great centre of the tanning trade, good leather such as War- rington tanned had been in good demand at a good price. Above all, the great cotton industry had con- tinued to flourish. Many mills were working full time and many of them, he understood, had work to keep them going for many months to come. Another industry with which, however, the Company was not much concerned, was the shipping industry and that had surpassed all previous records. Of course the very activity of such a trade had been beneficial to the iron and steel and other kindred trades which had supplied the materials for the shipping industry, but, on the other hand, the ship-owners found that the multiplica- tion of ships had told very heavily against the profits in the carrying trade and they were complaining of the low rate of freight. Two things would operate in the long run to check this large production of tonnage, for not only would the price of materials increase with the demand for new ships but every new ship put on the water would compete for the freights and so cause a still greater reduction in the freights. He regreted that they could not report any decided improvement in the two staple trades of Leicester, with which the bank was well connected—hosiery and the boot trades— indeed the future of the latter trade could not, he feared, be regarded with much confidenee. It was an undoubted fact that TRAMS AND EICYCLEa, now saved so much shoe leather as well as time as to seriously affect the normal demand for boots and shoes. It was a curious instance of how the social habits of a people might materially affect the demand for even so primitive and so universal a material as shoe leather. .(Laughter.) The iron and steel trades of course, were booming. The output from MiddlesbOrough alone for the last six months came to over 700,000 tons—a very good increase when compared with the 460.00 tons of the previous six months. At the present moment both Germany and America were buying iron and steel from us. A general survey—even though some of the trades with which they were connected were not doing well— certainly led one to believe that much money was being made both in the home and foreign trade, especially the latter. But there were still more lasting evidences for good which, if they became more pronounced, must conduce to the permanent benefit of our trading industry in this country. One of those symptoms was the

as the circulating medium. It was not perhaps gener- ally known that in this country nearly 98 per cent. of all our buying and selling was done without the need of money at all, and in that respect we were very much in I advance of other nations. Nowhere else was economy of gold brought to such perfection. Of necessity, of course, there was much more gold required as a circulat- ing medium in other countries but in time they would ' attain the same perfection of economy in that respect that we had arrived at. Our colonies were rapidly assimilating their practice to ours. He had been told ' some times that he said a great deal about other matter but very little about Parr's Bank, but after all the matters he had been referring to did concern Parr's bank as well as every other bank. (Hear, bear). He I could not go on for ten or fifteen minutes ; gilding the gold of their balance sheet. He would I leave the shareholders to paint the lily in their own way. (Laughter.) As compared with the balance sheet of a year ago, they showed an addition to their deposits of nearly a million. Their loans and advances, which were the least realisable portion of their assets, were less by more than £400,000. On the other hand their cash and short money and investments, which were the more realizable portion of their assets,werep in larger. That showed that they were n a more liquid condition, should anything happen. There had been a larger addition this half-year to the number of their customers' accounts,and an increase in the number of shareholders since July last of 180, making a total on the books of 7,786. Once again their bad debts were miraculously small, but when a miracle was repeated often it ceased to become a miracle. This was really no miracle at all but was entirely due to the exceptionally good manage- ment that Parr's Bank enjoyed. (Applause.) Mr. Parr concluded by moving the adoption of the report, and the declaration of a dividend at the rate of 19 per cent. per annum. Sir DAvID GAMBLE, Bart., seoonded, and gave some interesting extracts from the report presented at the first general meeting of the company forty years ago. The motion was unanimously carried. DIRECTORS THANKED. Mr. STONE moved a vote of thanks to the directors for their services during the year. Mr. DAVID GARNETT seconded, and hoped the Bank would long retain a strong body of Lancashire directors. The motion was heartily agreed to and the CHAIRMAN responded. They had, he said, plenty of directors who were not Lancashire men, but while they had a Lanca- shire lad for their general manager, Parr's Bank would not go very far wrong. (Applause.) THE STAFF THANKED. Mr. DIXON moved a vote of thanks to the general manager and officers for their services and said it was largely due to their activity that they had such a splendid balance sheet. Mr. JOEL WAINWRIGHT seconded and the motion was cordially passed. Mr. WHALLEy responded and spoke in very high terms of the r services of the staff which were summed up in the balance sheet, of which they were proud. (Applause) A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman on the proposition of Mr. J. C. STEAD, seconded by Mr. J. D. StICKTON, and Mr. PARR suitably acknowledged the compliment.

The Warrington innings totalled 143, the great feature being a dashing display by T. Wright, a promising young cricketer who made 31. Cresswell was also in capital form and ran up 21, J. Callon compiling 23. Bates was liarlestown's most successful bowler, captur- ing five wickets for 43.

Earlestown made a sturdy fight, a sound defence by Crawley (23) and Afarsh (24) putting a good complexion on the game from their point of view. With the intro- duction of Podmore, however, the end was hastened and the last man was dismissed when the aggregate had reached 113. Podmore did splendid rvice with the ball, taking three wickets for 13 runs.

The second team also added to their laurels, and they now hold the top position in the second division. Earlestown were accounted for in unmistakable fashion, the top scorers for Warrington being T. Houghton (12.), R. Carson (38) and A. Thewlis (34). Bennett bowled splendidly, and accounted for six wickets for 39 runs.

Peaketh Friends' School registered a victory at the expense of the Old Boys. J. W. Proud (2s) made the finest display for the winners, while G. Howard knocked up 29 for the vanquished.

St. James's had no difficulty in accounting for the "Guardian Office," the Rev. D. R. Davies taking five wickets for six runs, and Efolt four wickets for eight runs.

Milling and Co.'s employees visited the pretty village of Arley and defeated the local eleven, Sproson (17) being their top scorer.

St. James's 2nd had to bow the knee te Fairfield, but there was no particularly good scoring on either side.

The third round of the Works Competition is pro- gressing and on Tuesday evening Fleicher Russell's proved too good for Monks, Hall. The Liverpool-road team batted first and during their fifty minutes' stay at the wickets scored 57 for the loss of seven wickets. Baxter and Hughes showing good form for their runs. Ratcliffe took live wickets for 22 runs. In response Woodcock and Mason batted splendidly, making 24 and 19 (not out) respectively. Fletcher's won after losing Ithree wickets, and in fifty minutes scored 76.

COMPENSATION FOR THE LOSS OF AN EYE.

Unsuccessful Application by the Firth Company.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands K.C., the Firth Company Ltd., Florence Mills, Howley, steel wire manufacturers, applied that payments of 17s. per week which were being made by •them under a com- pensation award to Edward Hardmau, 31, Stafford-road, Wilderipool, should be terminated or diminished.—Mr, Adshead Elliott, barrister (instructed by Mr. J. W. Knowles) appeared for the applicants, and Mr. Acton, barrister (instructed by Mr. W. E. Foivler) for the respondents. Mr. Elliott said the accident in respect of which weekly payments were at present being made occurred on November 22nd last year. Respondent was follow- ing his employment when he was struck in the right eye by the end of apiece of wire which swung from a revolving cable. The injury was a very severe one and the right eye was permanently damaged, but the sight had not been altogether taken away. Some time ago the eye had, as the medical men termed it, quietened down and it was no source of danger to the left eye, which was a good sound one. Liability for the accident was immediately admitted and compensation was paid to the respondent at the rate of 17s. a week, as from a fortnight after the accident. To have the matter put in a formal way before the Court, an ' award was applied for and granted in March. Apart from the injury, respondent was a strong, sound healthy man of forty-five or forty-six years of age and as soon as his eye recovered as much as ever it would, efforts were made by his employers to get him to return to work. Respondent did go back on February 13th and he was put to the exact work that he had been doing up to the time of the accident. He worked from 9 in the morning until 5.30 in the afternoon and did the work perfectly satisfactorily. The only complaint he made, and which apparently prevented him from returning to work- the following morning WAS that during the day he almost injured two of his fingers, and that he was frightened to come back again. Mr. Elliott contended that respondent nearly hurt his fingers because he was doing a dangerous thing which was against the rules of wire-drawing. As Hardman did not return to work during that month and the following month, the firm thought some steps ought to be taken, and they arranged for him to be examined by Dr. Hamilton, eye specialist, Liverpool, who came to the conclusion that there was no reason whatever why he should not do exactly the same kind of work in which he was previously engaged. Respondent, however, said he would not run the risk of returning to work again. Of course he was entitled to say that he would not do the work, but if he was able to do it he was not entitled to a continuance of the compensation. The firm were willing to put Hardman to lighter work if he desired. Daniel Birrell, superintendout engineer to the Firth Company, gave the opinion that Hardman should be able to resume work stating that there was at present a man with one eye doing the same class of work satisfactorily. By Mr. Acton: He knew Hardman cut his finger whoa ho resumed work but be did not know he was ill for a fortnight. Mr. Samuel Taylor, managing director of the Firth Company, slid that if the respondent got over his nervousness be did not see why he should not do the work. Mr. Acton read a letter sent to the firm on behalf of the respondent stating that he was not able to follow his original occupation and expressing willingness to do other work if it were offered. Thomas Sutcliffe, wire drawer employed at the works said he lost the sight of his left eye when a boy, but by working carefully he was able to follow his employ- ment. He did not, however, think he would be able to do his work if he had lost his right eye. Dr. Langdale was of opinion that Hardman would be able to carry out his employment up to a certain extent, but he was not prepared to say that at present he could do it as efficiently. By I‘ll-. Acton : For all practical purposes the right eye was useless. Dr. Hamilton, eye specialist, Liverpool, said he thought Hardman could make an effort to resume work if he would and that in time he would be as good a workman as before.

Mr. Acton contended that the expert evidence was all in his favour, as it had not been proved that Hardman could do the work. He had always been willing to try his hand at any Work and he only gave up when his hand was cut. _

His Honour decided against the application with costs. At the same time he strongly recommended Hardman to make an effort to do something.

BANKRUPT DISCHARGED.

Successful Application at Warrington.

.--.. -A--

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday George Henry Holt made an application for his discharge from bankruptcy. Mr. Gibson, official receiver, said the receiving order against the applicant was made in 1904 upon Holt's own petition. The liabilities to rank for dividend were estimated at £357 15s. 3d.2 but actually amounted to £398 Ds. 2d. The bankrupt estimated his assets at £B9 15s. 2d., but actually realised £lB7 75., a dividend of 7s. lid. in the £ having been paid. Bankrupt com- menced business in partnership with his brother in November 1896 as a plumber, subsequently carrying on the business alone. Bankrupt's business had been quite free from any commercial irregularities and his only offence was .that he had not kept books of account.

Mr. Forshaw, who appearea for applicant: You can hardly expect a small tradesman to keep a chartered accountant on the premises. His Honour : Under the circumstances it is not to be expected. Mr. Gibson : I do not in this case regard the non- keeping of books as dishonest, but if a man desired to be dishonest the easiest way was for him not to keep books. . .

Bankrupt's immediate discharge was granted subject to his consenting to a judgment against him for £2O, the money tn be paid into Court within three months. Mr. Forshaw said the money would be paid.

MR. SEDDON M.P., MAY BE ARRESTED.

The Free Speech Question at Widnes.

Excitement at Widnes in connection with the free speech campaign is as intense as over, and the agitation has received fresh vigour from the arrest of two mem- bers of the Labour party there, On Thursday morning at three o'clock Peter N'Carthy Arthur Finchniau, and Patrick Hooligan, members of the Widnes Labour party, were roused from their beds and arrested in respect of unpaid fines of is. and costs for haying obstructed Victoria-square. Finchman paid the money at the last moment, but the other two were conveyed to gaol. iVrCarthy is evar sixty years of age. ' At noon an indignation meeting of the party WAS held, and a resolution passed condemning the action of the police. It was decided to pay AlTarthy's fine in consideration of his age. Both men had, however, been conveyed to Walton by that time. Particulars of the situation were telegraphed to Mr. Seddon, ALP., at the House of Commons. Mr. Seddon's fine still remains unpaid. He is duo at Widnes to speak , again this Saturday night, and public curiosity is much ' aroused in view of his possible arrest. Henry •Ackerley, a well-known local Labour man, was fined is. and costs for obstructing a meeting on July 8. He denied being present, and six witnesses swore that he was with them near the Grosvenor Hotel at the time of the meeting, but the Bench dedided to convict.

A HORSE TRANSACTION.

Peculiar Case at the County Court.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour, Judge Bowen Rowlands, ICC., Thomas Magina, horse dealer, 26, Norley-street, brought an action against Mr. Edward Connor, to recover the sum of £lO for the return or value of a horse, and damages for wrongful detention. Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, defendant being represented by Mr. E. A. Connor. Plaintiff's case was that on the 29th June he went to a horse fair in Ireland and there purchased some horses. Having settled for these he and the vendor adjourned for a drink and were followed by a man named Frank Duffey. Duffey said he wanted to get to England and asked Maginn to take him. Maginn refused to do so but Duffey got into the train along with plaintiff's horses and travelled to Dundalk without a ticket. The two not met at Greenore, and as free passes were granted to horse dealers Maginn, as an act of charity, obtained one for Duffey. Arrived at Warrington Duffey obtained work with Messrs. Maginn Brothers. Mr. T. Maginn on the Monday offered tba horses for sale to Mr. Edward Connor, but terms dbre not agreed upon. Subsequently plaintiff visited several public-houses and got drunk. In this state Duffey took him to Mr. Connor and, it was alleged by the defendant, he agreed to sell one of the horses for £2l. Plaintiff could not remember the transaction, and submitted that if such a bargain was made it was void, as he was not in a fit state to enter into a contract. The money was paid to Duffey, plaintiff had not yet received it. SCARCELY RF.EP PACE Evidence was called to show that the value with the world's requirements. WAVE OF TEMPERANCE

which at present was sweeping over the land and the second was the increased desire and readiness shown on the part of masters and men alike to settle their differ- ences other than by the ruinous and barbarous methods of strikes and lockouts. Owing to the adoption of sliding scales and the action of conciliation boards, and in a great measure, to private arrangements, many cases were settled and disputes arranged with a minimum of friction to all concerned. One case alone this half year, which involved the well-being of 150,000 miners, was settled by a conciliation board. Perhaps he would be asked why, if times were so good, did they read so much in the papers about stagnation and depression They were not all in the city of London, happily. It , was true that complaints loud and deep came from the Stock Exchange. No business they said, and that complaint found an echo on the provincial stock exchanges of Liverpool and Manchester. " Specula- tion " they said " is dead," and speculation was the life of the Stock Exchange. He feared that showed for one thing what a small percentage of business on the Stock Exchange was due to hona-fide investment, and how much to speculation. It was not, however, quite true that speculation was dead, human nature being still very much as it was, or even that there was no money considered that and all the trade de eisgpiellcvLsl countries etarsi ews igt aohno. dd Wo hau as it n theyit Coloniesa s in which we had so much money invested were doing so well, there must certainly be a great deal of money coming back to this country in investments, dividends and pro- fits, some of which doubtless was put into bona Pax investments, Much of it also was doubtless put into extension of the business but certainly a great deal was loft to speculation. He was inclined to think not that speculation was dead but that it was merely taking just now another form. Instead, of buying,for -instance, , existing securities on the Stock Exchange for a rise, he thought people were putting their money into new ventures—still a speculation to a certain extent. He saw it stated the other day that no less than 233 MILLIONS or MONEY had been put into electrical undertakings alone in the course of the last ten years. Shares such as these were not to a very great extent applied for or taken up to be sold on the first rise, but they were held for better or worse in the hope of future dividends. Still this • putting of money into comparatively new industries was in a measure speculatien. As an instance he might fairly cite the motor bus industries into which several millions of money had been put in the last twelve months. While putting money into new industries was obviously not so acceptable a form of spectulation to the stock broker, he was quite sure it was much more restful to the speculator himself, worn out by the long drawn misery of his South African experiences. Talking of South Africa, it would seem to make the present lot of that land harder still when they turned to our other colonies and found them having exceptionally prosper- ous times. The very name of Canada nas a

SYNONYM FOR PROSPERITY. Their only fear was that she should go too fast and incur the inevitable punishment of reaction. They must all hope that she would make haste slowly. As regarded Australia it would appear that she really had more money than she knew what to do with. The banks in the two colonies of New South Wales and Victoria , alone added four millions iii Victoria and Sidney to ;their stock on account and not only had they done this I but they had put eight millions more to their credit in ' London. All that was newly-acquired Wealth. Ti they compared the March quarter for 1905 with that of the ; present year, the imports of the two-colonies showed an ; increase of 10 per cent., while their exports I showed an increase, excluding gold, of 32 per I cent. and with gold of 04 per cent. This ;prosperity they might see in another way by studying ; the bank balance sheets, which showed a reduction of 223 millions in loans and advances to customers, while - I the deposits had increased:by 11 millions. If they added the latter sum to the above extinction oil iabilities,which ; was as good as an asset of 2; millions„ it showed more : than thirteen millions of wealth added to those colonies in the last few years. In spite of alk her troubles South Africa continued to break the record for gold production. It now amounted to two milliods a month; but, unfortunately, no one seemed One penny the better , for it out there. Here the mining shares continued to ; fall. A. very slight rise—a momentary one he feared— was recorded that morning, but they had continued to fall for many months, and out there men were starving. Astonishment was often expressed by the "loan in the , street "how it was that with such an enormous annual ; supply—it now reached a total of 75 millons per annum —there should be enough gold and plenty of it to go round. Well, he (Mr. Parr) was no expert on this ques- tion, but when they considered the development and extension of the world's industries the ever-increasing volume of its wealth and the greater and more extended , distribution of that wealth, and finally the increase in . the world's population, he was certainly led to believe that the present production of gold, enormous as it ; seemed, could

Bowling.

The third round in connection with the Warrington Works Competition was completed on Saturday, the match which was decided producing one of the closest struggles in the history of the competition. The com- peting teams were Sankey Wire Mills and Monks Hall (1), and the Wirepullers gained a substantial advantage as the result of the first game only to have their lead wrested from them immediately. They looked to J. I Foster to retrieve their position but he failed to do so. 'Starting badly he allowed his opponent to reach 9-4. He then put in a good run but had to retire defeated. Two of the eight points which separated the teams at this stage, were wiped off by G. Rutter who after being in arrears 9-1 with J. Heywood, won splendidly 15.13.

Then followed one of the best exhibitions of the match. Dividing the first two points T. Cooper ran away from Bennett 14-5, and Sankey looked with con- fidence to the lead being reversed. They had, however, reckoned without Bennett, who secured the flock and ran home whilst Cooper marked time. The next game saw Sankey left still further behind, and a victory of 15-13, which J. Graham scored over R. Jefferson, did not occasion any consternation in the Monks Hall camp. and S. Deakin promptly replied by defeating Goulden 15-14, the totals standing at 106-9 G.

It was now that Sankey began to assert themselves and J. Harrison after leading throughout against T. Rigby brought the teams within one point of each other. Another victory to Monks Hall raised high their hopes of victory, but a lead of seven points was not sufficient. ,S. Fletcher and R. Woods were the next pair on the 1 green and Fletcher quickly running from 8-3 to 15-9 one point only separated the teams. Naturally the greatest interest centered in the last game which lay between W. Foster and F. Sraelley, and Foster quickly raised the spirits of the Sankey men by scoring seven before his opponent could get a single. The promise of the first few ends was not broken and Sankey rejoiced in a victory won only in the last minute.

The first round for the consolation in connection with the Works competition prizes was opened on Saturday when four matches were decided. The teams were limited to six aside and exciting finishes could be looked for. The expectations of the spectators were fully realized in the match between Greenings, and Thewlis and Griffiths. Greenings won the match in the first few minutes as W. Patrick all but clean plated Dagnall, and gave his side a lead of 14 points. Thewlis and Griffiths set themselves manfully to the task of wiping off the arrears and gradually improved their position, but the wet* of pulling the game out of the fire was beyond them and they Jost by three points.

Monks Hall (2) lost to Crosflelds (2) in exactly the same way, their first trundler retiring defeated 15-1. The remainder of the games they won but as in the other case the opening margin against them proved too great. The third contingent from the" soapery " gave a truly inglorious display and were beaten by Whitecross (1) by 00-27.

Cycling. In the Athletic News under the heading of " A coming champion " " Cyclops "says : The unexpected opposition offered by 'Charles Berry against Ernest Payne and his defeat of Lavery and Baker in the purenit ' race at the Manchester Wheelers' meet has brought the Warringtonian into the front rank at one hound. Short and sturdy, with an abundance of vitality and pluck, Berry is the beau ideal of a short distance sprinter, his almost perfect ankle action and a style free from any jarring digs or lunges forming an object lesson in the art of pedalling. Although only 24 years of age. Berry has crowded into his short career enough victories to satisfy the appetite of any two men, and if I were to predict for him the highest honours that the cycling world has to offer I should not be exaggerating his possibilities. For two years in succession he has captured the Manchester blue riband of the path—the mile centre championship, and has this year placed his name on the Cawley and Wallasey cups. After a perusal of his long list of successes I might point out that he has on one occasion secured the much-coveted Duke of Portland watch at the Worksop gale. Riding in the colours of the Salford Harriers ho was one of the favoured invited competitors in the now famous Muratti Cup race, but owing to an unfortunate accident in the last lap— his chain left the cogs—he was put out of the race. A non-smoker and very temperate in his habits, Berry divides his training between the cinder track at Winnington Park and riding on the road,and the wonder of it is that with such poor facilities for getting fit he should develop such an exceptional sprint. With a cement track to train upon this pretty perlaller would make a good show in the very best company.

===== 0002601_19060728_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
Swimming.

The second round of the works squadron competition for the Sir Gilbert Greenall challenge shield was entered upon in the Corporation Baths, Legh-street. on Thursday evening before a fair attendance of spectators.: The first teams to swim off were Corporation and Cros- fields (2). On the first man Corporation gained half a length, and this lead was increased, and in the end they won by over a length. Monks Hall and Richmonds were more evenly matched, but with two men out of the water Monks Hall held the advantage, and almost half a length in their favour separatecrthe teams at the end of the race. Last year's winners (Crosfields No.l team) were pitted against P. and, K., who held the shield the first year it was competed for. The race was full of excitement, and was won by P. and K. by four yards. On Thursday night Warrington met St. Helens in connection with the Liverpool and District League, in the Warrington baths. In the first half the home team defended the deep end. For a time play was very even when Warrington broke away and after being hard pressed Slater was beaten by Allen, but a minute later Lomax waiting well down the bath. equalized Warring- ton were having the best of matters but were unable to improve their position and the teams changed ends with the scores equal.

Re-starting Warrington at once took up the attack but good work by Slater prevented them scoring. Walker had a splendid opening but failed to take advan- tage of it. It was left to Peers to give the home team the lead that player putting the finishing touch to a passing movement initiated by Pemberton. A minute later St. Helens were penalized in their own goal and Ashton further improved the Warrington lend. Final : ,Warrington three goals, St. Helens one goal.

Warrington thoroughly deserved their victory, and had Slater been less active in goal the home team's score would have been even greater. Whilst they were the best team, the Wirepullers however lacked combina- tion, and the paying of greater attention to this depart- ment will doubtless result in the winning of more matches. C. Ashton again stood out as the best player in the water.

While Henry J. Hickman, a Hornsey gardener, was digging some potatoes three weeks ago the fork slipped, and, penetrating his boot, injured his foot. Some days later be was found to be suffering from all the symptoms of tetanus, and on Tuesday last he succumbed.—At the inquest the jury returned a verdict of " Death by mis- adventure."

The usual meeting was held on Sunday, when Mr Wooley presided, and gave a short, earnest address. Mr. C. F. Fielding, of Liverpool, spoke on the question of the Man in the Gospel : Master, what good thing must I do to inherit eternal life ?" He pointed out how Christ answered the question by relating the story of the good Samaritan who, in contrast to the priest and the Levite, extended his help to the wounded way- farer. Christ gave the injunction to"Go andldo likewise." The speaker referred to the need around them of extend- ing a helping bond, a loving wish, and a great care for the fallen. The earne-Aness and the deep feeling of the speaker greatly impressed the meeting, Mr. W. H. Payton, Mug. Bac., presided at the organ. Miss Jessie Farrar sang sweetly "Come unto Me " and "The Star of Bethlehem." The organ fund contribution was lis Bd.-51r. G. Colley and Mr. Joseph Owe,, also took part in the meeting.-The Secretary announced that next Sunday the open meeting would be held in Winwick-street Cooperative Hell at 3 p.m., and that the meetings would be held there until the re-opening of the Church. LIVERPOUL FIRE BBIGADE. The Fire Department of our principal seaport is one of the best equipped in the kingdom. It has the most powerful portable land steam fire engine yet built and, to enable fires to be attended with great promptness, is adopting motor engines. The fourth Alerryvieethe " Fire King " self propelling steamer has just beer delivered to Liverpool and, like two of the others, in fitted with a first aid chemical cylinder as well as with fire pumps throwing two tors of water per minute..

A Handy Touring Holdall, which is illustrated above, came under this writer's observation. It consisted of an outside cover of waterproof tweed, 14 in. wide and 28 in. long, with straps for closing it up and fastening to the saddle springs under the tool bag, which is left out of alas illustration to better indicate the means of stied'. rnent, which is by small straps fastened%on to a leather-covered, metal strip connecting the tow straps. This cost 2s. 6d., and in its original for contained no fittings of any use except the folding over flaps at each side. Specially small shaving and toilet kit were purchased and fitted into place by strips of elastic, pockets for spare stockings, handkerchiefs, shirt, Ste., were sewn in, and a thin jacket carried in the centre .enatiled the whole to be bound up intb a convenient symmetrical package. The Concealment of Weights. • It is to be regretted that so many makers of bicycles do not recognize the wisdom of publishing in their catalogues the weights of their various models. It may be argued that the variations ha frame sizes, tyres, and other details render this course inconvenient, and that an exact statement of the weight of a roadster for everyday riding, sold at a popular price, is not a matter of concern! to the intending buyer. But allowing for this I objection as regards the cheaper models, it seems 1 unreasonable that the public should be in the dark as to the weight of machines deigned fot pleasure, riding, touring, and light road work. It would be useful to the purchaser if he knew the weight of a 24 in. frame machine built to a given specification, as he could then form an idea of the increase or reduction involved by any alterations of frame, wheels, tyres, &c., deemed necessary: Of course, the particulars may be obtained through an agent, or on application to the makers, but surely the 1 aim of a catalogue should be to present complete. data respecting any particular machine to the slink cristinating reader.

THE NATIONAL ScHooL.—After negotialons extend- ing over three years the managers of the Padgate National School have at length completed the purchase of a piece of laud at the rear of the school premises at a cost of over £9O, for the extension of the school yards. Owing to the small area of the pla:vground the children have hitherto been allowed to play on the road, and, as several minor accidents have occurred,the managers have been anxious to provide ample space cchere the scholars could play in safety, hence the addition. It .is expeztted that, with the laying out, nailing in, and provision of new offices a further ex- pnditure of about two hundred pounds will be necessary. SEPARATION Ar LER ONLY TWO YP.AftS. After two years of married life Blanche Snowl.4ll, of Leinster Gardens, sued her husband at Runcorn on Monday, for a separation order on the ground of deser-. tion. In Whit-week he came home drunk, his mother- in-law upbraided him, and he left the house, and did not return. Since then he had not sent her a penny. He earned from ,C 2 10s. to £3 10s. per week.—ln June this year her husband wrote and asked her ta meet him at I Manchester to make further arrangements. Sue met him, and he left her just outside Manchester Central ! Station, went into a public-house, and gave her the: slip.—The magistrates suggested a conference, and I evenstrally a separation order was agreed to that the I defendant should pay his wife 10s. per week and the costs, £2 19s. 6d.
